Only when the engine serves no pages does the step carry a link: the one the request handed over (the long link within the budget, else the hash link; [The request file and pasted blocks](#the-request-file-and-pasted-blocks)). `revertUrl` and `requestFile` stay absent: the agent sent the file with the request. `EXPIRED` says nothing was reverted (or, for a signed plan not finished in time, what was) and that a new request is needed.
- **Delivering the signature.** `signature` is the `basalt-revert.1.` code the revert page shows the person (a revert response, [Lines, binding and codes](#lines-binding-and-codes)), for when the page cannot relay it: the engine does not serve the local pages, or the person reaches the agent only through a chat and has no terminal. Like `write`'s `approval`, the engine checks the code is for `revertId`, checks it locally against the registered passkeys, and submits it with `apply_revert` as `basalt revert --apply` does; Postgres verifies the passkey signature. A revert of up to 1000 lines is then applied (`APPLIED`); a large plan's signature is recorded and the agent applies it in batches (`APPLYING`). A code for another revert, a bad signature or rows changed since the preview is `REVERT_INVALID`; an expired revert is `REVERT_EXPIRED`. The code is the person's signature over that revert's binding (its id, `rowsHash` and expiry): an agent holding it can submit that exact revert or not, nothing else. With `signature` the call does not wait (`waitForSignature` or `reissueLink: true` with it is `INVALID_ARGUMENT`). A `signature` that does not decode as a revert code (an approval code, for one) is `INVALID_ARGUMENT`; the engine also refuses with `REVERT_INVALID`, before Postgres, a code signed by a passkey that is not registered or was revoked, one that does not verify against the revert's binding, and one for a revert already signed (a replay); `REVERT_EXPIRED` for an expired revert. Reasons, for the [next steps](#next-steps-per-code): `BAD_SIGNATURE` (another revert's code, or one that does not verify), `PASSKEY_REVOKED` (unknown or revoked passkey), `PLAN_FINISHED` (already signed), `ROWS_CHANGED` (Postgres: rows changed since the preview), `NOT_SIGNED` or `NOT_FINISHED` for `REVERT_EXPIRED`. Postgres checks every code again; nothing is written on any refusal.
- **One wait per turn**, as for the [waiting write](#approvals): the agent shows `personMessage`, then in the same turn calls the wait form once. When it returns `PENDING`, its `personMessage` and `nextSteps` tell the agent not to wait again but to end its turn with the link and the expiry; when the person says they signed, a later call with `revertId` returns at once. MCP cannot start a client turn when the person signs, so a finished turn is not woken.
- **Receipt** (VC1-17). An `APPLIED` or `APPLYING` answer of the wait form, after a wait or after delivering `signature`, carries the engine's `receipt` (or `noReceipt`), with the receipt line first in `personMessage` ([Receipts](#receipts)).
- Nothing in either form signs a revert, and nothing applies one without the person's signature: only their passkey signs, checked inside Postgres; `apply_revert_batch` still runs only lines of a plan the person signed.

**Revert scope on `preview_revert` and `request_revert`** (in part served; owner decisions RS-1 to RS-7, which replace M3-14 and narrow N12-R7; needs `basalt_meta` [version 20](#version-20-revert-scope)). The agent proposes, the person chooses ([Rescope, widen and reject](#rescope-widen-and-reject)). Served: every bullet below, including the wait form's `REJECTED` and widened answers and the delivery of a widened revert's signature. Only `select` in the results is planned.

- **Own work only** (served). Both tools take exactly one of `changeId`, `runId` or `taskId` (`request_revert` gains `taskId`, RS-7), and the scope must be the agent's own: its change (`HasChange` on its view), its run (`FindRun`), or a task it ran in (it has a run with that task id in `agent_runs`; any run counts). The engine checks this first, for every scope kind and on both tools, before any guard call; Postgres checks it again ([version 20](#version-20-revert-scope)). Another agent's change or run is `NOT_FOUND` reason `CHANGE` or `RUN`; a task the agent never ran in is `NOT_FOUND` reason `TASK`. In a task the proposal holds only the agent's own lines: a row another agent changed later in the task is skipped as `CHANGED_AFTER`. `request_revert`'s refusal of `taskId` and the `NOT_FOUND`-only-when-empty special case of `preview_revert` go.
- **Filters** (served). Both tools gain optional `runs` (only with `taskId`: 1 to 20 of the agent's own runs with that task id) and `tables` (any scope: 1 to 20 `schema.table` names, each a guarded table the agent wrote in the scope). Absent means all. No tool takes `agents`: the engine builds the [scope descriptor](#the-scope-descriptor) itself, with `agents` = `[the agent's role]`. A filter entry outside the agent's own work (another agent's run, an own run outside the task, `runs` without `taskId`, a table it did not write in the scope) is `INVALID_ARGUMENT` reason `SCOPE_FILTER`; the message names each refused entry with one fixed text per kind, so another agent's run id reads exactly like an unknown run id, and a table that does not exist, is not guarded, or was not written by the agent in the scope reads "`<table>` has no change of yours in this scope". More than 20 runs or tables is `SCOPE_FILTER_CAP`. Filters that leave no line are `NOTHING_TO_REVERT` with the AE-8 reason for what is left.
- **Results.** Planned: both tools' results gain `select`, the normalized descriptor. Served: `preview_revert` of an own task is `PREVIEW_TASK` with the new steps in [Next steps per code](#next-steps-per-code); its `personMessage`, and `request_revert`'s for a task, always add "You can include other agents' changes in this task on the signing page.", the same sentence whether or not anyone else wrote in the task. An own task with nothing to undo is `NOTHING_TO_REVERT` with the AE-8 reason even when other agents' changes could be reverted; its `personMessage` then names `basalt revert --agent <name> --task <id> --open`, which opens the page on an empty proposal for the person to widen (RS-6); the same command is a `PERSON` `RUN_COMMAND` step after the `STOP` ([Next steps per code](#next-steps-per-code), task scope), so with a long `--config` path the message says "the command the agent shows you" in its place.
- **Wait form.** Served: status gains `REJECTED` (with `rejectedAt` and the person's `note` when they wrote one; steps: `STOP`), returned at once once the person rejected it. Served: for a revert the person widened, every answer carries `widened: true` and only `revertId`, `status`, `intent`, `batched`, `rows`, `skipped`, `expiresAt`, `personMessage` (which starts "You changed the scope on the signing page; the agent cannot see what you added.") and, for a large plan, `changeId`, `applyBy` and `progress`: no `scope`, `scopeId`, `select`, lines or link. A widened `PENDING` answer has the `WAIT_ENDED` steps with no URL: the person signs on the page they widened on, and the `reissueLink` step says it resets the revert to the agent's own work. No answer for a widened revert names its scope in `personMessage` (it says "the revert you widened on the signing page"). A step that requests or prepares the revert again (`EXPIRED`, `ROWS_CHANGED`) names the revert's scope kind and id with no filters: a change or run of the agent's, or a task it ran in (the person widens a run at most to its task), so the request covers only the agent's own work and the agent learns nothing it did not have. `APPLYING` keeps the batch loop, by line numbers. `reissueLink: true` on a widened revert resets it to the agent's proposal ([Link secrets](#link-secrets)).
- **Delivering a widened revert's signature** (served). The agent role cannot read a widened revert's binding text, so for `signature` on a widened revert the engine checks only that the code names this `revertId`, the revert is `PENDING` and the credential id is a registered, unrevoked passkey, and leaves the signature check to `apply_revert`, mapping its refusal to the same codes and reasons as today. Those refusals' `OPEN_LINK` steps carry no URL for a widened revert: the agent holds no link to it. When `apply_revert` finds no row to restore in the signed scope (`NOTHING_TO_REVERT`), the engine answers `NOTHING_TO_REVERT` reason `ALL_CHANGED_BY_PEOPLE` when the scope has skipped lines, else `READS_ONLY`, with its own message instead of the guard's detail (which names the scope). What is applied does not change.

**Reverts in `list_pending`** (served; owner decisions LP-1 to LP-3, Nov 13). The person's pending list carries reverts as well as held writes.

- **One list (LP-1).** `list_pending` returns one `items` list. Each item has `kind`: `APPROVAL` (a held write, every field as before) or `REVERT` (below); the schema is a `oneOf` on `kind`, so each kind keeps its own required fields. Items of both kinds are in one order by id (UUIDv7, so by time; `sort` `id` or `-id`, default `-id`) with one cursor. There is no compatibility shim: approval items gain `kind` and nothing else changes for them.
- **Arguments.** `kind` (new, optional): `APPROVAL` or `REVERT`, default both. `status`: `PENDING` (both kinds: waiting for the person), `APPROVED` (held writes only: approved, the agent applies them) or `APPLYING` (reverts only: a signed large plan the agent applies in batches); default all three. `runId` filters held writes by run and lists no revert. `limit`, `cursor`, `sort` and `reissueLink` as before.
- **Which reverts.** The calling agent's own reverts (`agent_reverts`) with status `PENDING` (not expired) or `APPLYING`. An applied, expired or rejected revert is not listed: the wait form of `request_revert` reports it.
- **A revert item.** `kind: "REVERT"`, `revertId`, `status` (`PENDING` or `APPLYING`), `intent`, `rows`, `skipped`, `batched`, `widened`, `createdAt`, `expiresAt`, `headline`, `personMessage`, `nextSteps` (a `PENDING` revert: the person opens the link, then the agent calls `request_revert` with `revertId` and `waitForSignature: 0` once they say they signed; an `APPLYING` one: `revert_plan` with `pending: true`), and when they apply `scope` and `scopeId` (absent when widened), `revertUrl` and `links` (a `PENDING` revert not widened: its long link, or with `reissueLink` a newly issued short link when the engine serves the pages), `applyBy` and `progress` (a signed large plan). `headline` is built from what Postgres returns, never from the agent's text: for a revert not widened "Revert of <scope> <id>[, only <filters>]: N rows to restore, M left as they are (changed after the agent)."; for a widened one only "Revert widened by the person: N rows to restore." with no scope, select, lines or link (RS-5).
- **`reissueLink: true`** issues a new short link for each `PENDING` revert item as for held writes; for a widened revert it first resets it to the agent's proposal (`reset_revert`, as `request_revert` does, [Link secrets](#link-secrets)), so the item then reads as not widened, with the link for the proposal.
- **Notifications (LP-2, parked).** The engine sends the person no notification. The Telegram notifier is parked outside the engine until after M5; notification channels come back later as plugins. The engine still builds a notice when `request_revert` prepares a revert (`REQUESTED`), when it issues a revert link again on the agent's request (`reissueLink: true` on `request_revert` or `list_pending`), and when it holds a write: the agent, the `headline` (as above), the intent, the expiry, the long link and, when the engine serves the pages, the short link; a notice about a widened revert carries only its headline (LP-3). It hands the notice to a notifier that does nothing, the seam a channel plugs into.

`write` in M2: two optional arguments, `approvalId` (uuid) and `approval` (an approval response code, below; needs `approvalId`). To apply a held write the agent repeats the same `sql`, `params`, `intent` and `runId` and adds `approvalId`; with `approval` the engine first submits the person's signed approval (the agent relays it; it cannot make one). The `APPLIED` result of a held write carries the engine's `receipt` (or `noReceipt`) and `personMessage`, the receipt line ([Receipts](#receipts)). A different statement, parameters or run under the approval is `APPROVAL_INVALID`; an approval not yet signed is `APPROVAL_INVALID` with a message saying so. A held write returns `APPROVAL_REQUIRED` with `approval` in the error body; the held change record has status `APPROVAL_PENDING` until it is applied (the record is then replaced by the `APPLIED` one, which carries `approvalId`) or expires (`APPROVAL_EXPIRED`). The ledger keeps the `APPROVAL_PENDING` and `APPROVED` rows either way.

- **Lists** are cursor-paginated (keyset, no offset): arguments `limit` (default 50, max 200, clamped by the handler) and `cursor` (opaque base64url); result `{ "items": [...], "nextCursor": "..." }`, `nextCursor` absent on the last page. Unknown arguments are rejected with `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, never ignored.
- One named params struct per list tool, embedding `pagination.Params`, passed by value through every layer; the same struct backs the CLI flags.
- Agents cannot approve or sign a revert through MCP. Approve and revert need a passkey signature checked inside Postgres. `apply_revert_batch` only runs lines of a plan the person already signed. `request_revert` prepares a revert, waits for the person's signature, and may deliver the person's signed `basalt-revert.1.` code (`signature`), as `write` delivers `approval`; an agent can never sign a revert, only deliver the person's signature.

### Large values

Owner decision of 14 Nov 2026: "make agents' mistakes reversible; if we keep hiding things, it's scary". A value can be far larger than an agent should read in one call: a change whose old and new rows hold 15 MB each, written by the agent itself. The rules split by what the agent does with the value.

- **`query` never shortens.** A row over the result budget is refused with `QUERY_TIMEOUT` `ROW_TOO_LARGE` ([Error codes](#error-codes)), naming the columns and sizes. The agent reads what it asked for, whole, or nothing: it never holds a shortened value it could write back as if it were the real one.
- **The ledger tools mark, they do not fail.** `run_history`, `list_pending` (a held write's `statement` and `params`), `preview_revert` and `revert_plan` read history the agent cannot change, and a large value must not stop the agent from paging it or from applying a signed revert. They replace a large value with a marker and return everything else of the item: ids, cursor, line numbers, op, table, outcome and the other values. A revert never runs from what the agent read: Postgres applies it from the stored rows, so a marker changes nothing about what is restored.
- **Cost.** Both caps below are measured in the budget's own unit, computed in Postgres before anything is read: a value costs the bytes of its JSON text as Postgres prints it (a string with its quotes and escapes, which is what a read of its image carries), plus 6 for every one of those bytes JSON escapes (a control byte, `"`, `\`, `<`, `>`, `&`), plus 128, plus its column's name. An image costs the sum of its values. So the caps bound what the engine is charged, not only the text: 1 000 000 `<` characters cost about 7 MB, not 1 MB.
- **Which values.** In each item, in order (a change record: `statement`, `params`, then each `rowChanges` entry by `seq`, its `key`, `before`, `after`; a revert line: `key`, `now`, `set`):
  1. A single value that costs over **1 MiB** (1 048 576) is left out: its column is absent from its image, never `null`. It is named in `largeValues`.
  2. Then an image (`key`, `before`, `after`, `now`, `set`, the `statement` or the whole `params`) is kept while the item's running cost stays at most **8 MiB** (8 388 608). The running cost counts the image's kept values and the `largeValues` entries of the values left out of it. An image that would take it over is left out whole (absent, never `null`) and named in one `largeValues` entry without `column`, which replaces the entries for its values. The images after it are tested the same way.

  The arithmetic, in the budget's unit:
  - The kept values and their entries cost at most 8 MiB.
  - A whole-image entry costs under 1 KiB. A `run_history` record is an agent change of at most 50 rows, cascades included (a revert's own record belongs to no run, so `run_history` never returns it), so its entries cost under 152 KiB. A revert line has 3 images, so its entries cost under 3 KiB.
  - A revert line's `changeIds` names every change that touched its row, without bound (one counter updated many times). On agent reads a line carries at most its first **100** `changeIds`, with `moreChangeIds` counting the rest; Postgres slices the array before it is read (the read is charged), so the field costs under 6 KiB.
  - The short fields cost under 1 MiB: the record's ids, op, table, outcome, counts and an intent of at most 500 characters, each of at most 50 `rowChanges` entries' own short fields (`table`, `op`, flags, `basedOn`), and a held write's `headline` and `assignedColumns`.
  - Building a link reads the item's request (binding text and lines) once more. Rule 1 below caps that read at **4 MiB** in the budget's unit, and the link fields it produces (`approvalUrl` or `revertUrl`, `links`, a `requestFile` of at most 1 MiB of text) are charged within it. An approval's binding text repeats `statement` and `params`, which is why the cap covers the binding too.

  So an item costs under 14 MiB (8 + 4 + 1 + the entries). Every page fits the 16 MiB budget at `limit` 1, and a `SIZE_LIMIT` ends with a smaller `limit`. The rules depend only on the item, never on the page, so an item reads the same at every `limit`. An image with `largeValues` is not the whole row: never rebuild a row or a write from it.
- **The marker.** `largeValues` ([`large-value.schema.json`](api/mcp/values/large-value.schema.json)), on a `run_history` record and on each `rowChanges` entry, a `list_pending` held-write item, a `preview_revert` item and a `revert_plan` line's `plan`; absent when nothing was left out. Each entry: `in` (`KEY`, `BEFORE`, `AFTER`, `NOW`, `SET`, `STATEMENT` or `PARAMS`), `column` (the column; for `PARAMS` there is `index`, 0-based, instead; absent when the whole image was left out), `bytes` (the size of the value's text, for the person; the caps use its cost) and `sha256` (lowercase hex of the SHA-256 of that text). A `params` array that has any value costing over 1 MiB is left out whole (one entry per large parameter, with its `index`), so the agent never resends a parameter list with a hole in it. The text of a value: a JSON string's characters in UTF-8 (no quotes or escapes); any other value its `jsonb` text as Postgres prints it; an image, its `jsonb` text; the statement, its text. Postgres computes `bytes` and `sha256` itself (for a string `convert_to(v #>> '{}', 'UTF8')`, else `convert_to(v::text, 'UTF8')`), so the large value never reaches the engine on an agent's call. Shared vectors (a string, an object with numerics such as `1.10` and a large integer, and a value with escapes, each with its `bytes` and `sha256` as Postgres computes them) are in [`api/mcp/values/large-value.json`](api/mcp/values/large-value.json), with each value's text and its cost in a named column; the engine's tests run them against Postgres, the CLI's cost test and the pages run them too.
- **Requests too large for a link.** A held write's or revert's long link and request file carry its lines whole. The pages decode only what [Format 2](#format-2-the-compact-request) allows: the payload, the inflated request and the rebuilt texts each at most 1 MiB. The request file's 4 MiB cap is above that. Every result that hands over or restates such a link (`write`'s hold, `list_pending`, `request_revert`, `revert_plan`) follows these rules:
  1. The engine first measures in Postgres every text the Format 2 request carries: the binding text and the lines, both in the budget's cost unit and as text. When the text is over **1 MiB** or the cost is over **4 MiB**, it reads none of them on the agent's call, and rule 3 applies.
  2. Otherwise it reads them and builds the Format 2 request. It then applies the page's three limits exactly. When any one fails, the request is too large for a long link, a hash link or a request file, and rule 3 applies.
  3. A request too large for a link is handed over only on a short link that can carry it ([The link budget](#the-link-budget), step 1). That means the engine's local pages (no size limit beyond the pages API), or the portal's short link over the Vault (the engine does not yet hand a request too large for a link over the direct transport's short link, a follow-up) when the lines cost at most **15 MiB** in the budget's unit, which counts the 6 bytes a JSON-escaped character takes in the page's response. The page's response over the Vault is at most 16 MiB ([Limits](#limits)), and the rest of it fits in the remaining 1 MiB. A field that would restate the long link carries the page's address with no fragment (`<base>/approvals/<approvalId>`, `<base>/reverts/<revertId>`), and `personMessage` points to the link already given.
  4. With no such short link, nothing can bring the request to a page: the CLI prints or opens the same long link. The result says so:
     - `write`'s hold is `APPROVAL_REQUIRED` reason `REQUEST_TOO_LARGE`, with the approval's `approvalId` and expiry as for any hold, and no link.
     - `request_revert` is `QUERY_TIMEOUT` reason `REQUEST_TOO_LARGE`. Its message names the prepared `revertId`, which `list_pending` (kind `REVERT`) also lists.
     - A `list_pending` item or `revert_plan` result has a link field with the page's address with no fragment, no `requestFile`, and a `personMessage` and `nextSteps` as for `REQUEST_TOO_LARGE`.
  5. The `REQUEST_TOO_LARGE` `personMessage` depends on the setup:
     - With a local `pagesUrl`: turn on the local pages (`pagesAddr` in `engine.json`, then restart the engine).
     - With the portal: turn on the Vault (`basalt init ... --vault=on`). A request too large for a link does not go over the direct transport's short link yet, so the message does not offer it.
     - When the lines cost over 15 MiB, only the local pages carry them. On an engine the person runs, that means a local `pagesUrl` with `pagesAddr`, and a passkey registered for that local origin (a portal passkey does not sign there). An engine that cannot serve them (an agent-operated engine on the portal: local pages need `devLocalPages`) can never get such a hold approved. The message says so plainly: the hold cannot be approved on this setup unless the setup changes, and otherwise it expires.
     - For a revert, in every case, the alternative is a narrower revert (`runs`, `tables`). The agent requests it again with `request_revert`.

     The steps are A `ASK_PERSON`, A `END_TURN`, then A `CALL_TOOL` once the person says they changed the setup. For a revert the call is `request_revert` with `{revertId, reissueLink: true}` (it returns at once; `waitForSignature` is not allowed with it): that item only, so no other revert is reset and no other link replaced. For a hold it is `list_pending` with `{kind: APPROVAL, status: PENDING, runId, reissueLink: true}`; this also gives the run's other pending holds new links, and the agent shows each item's `personMessage` as usual. On the `request_revert` error, a `STOP` comes first: do not retry now. The `ASK_PERSON` text asks the person to tell the agent before opening the revert, since the reissue resets a widened revert. When the person chooses a narrower revert instead, the `ASK_PERSON` step's text tells the agent to request it with `request_revert` and `runs` or `tables`. The setup change is a step for whoever runs the engine (`--vault=on`); there is no `RUN_COMMAND` variant for an agent-operated engine yet, so it stays under `ASK_PERSON`. `request_revert`'s wait form with `reissueLink` on such a revert answers the results case `REQUEST_TOO_LARGE`, not the error. The steps of `list_pending` items, `revert_plan` and `request_revert`'s wait in this case are in the results table ([Next steps per code](#next-steps-per-code), case `REQUEST_TOO_LARGE`).
  6. The held write and the prepared revert stay until they expire. The held write's preview lines count toward the budget only when they are read, so a held `DELETE` of a row with a large value is never refused with `SIZE_LIMIT`.
  7. A hold keeps its preview lines whole, up to **64 MiB** (67 108 864 bytes) of their text in all ([Version 37](#version-37-held-writes-keep-large-lines)): a held `DELETE` of a row with a large value is held, shown, signed and applied like any other. Over 64 MiB the write is refused with `STATEMENT_REFUSED` reason `HOLD_TOO_LARGE`, and the person is told to make the change themselves or have the agent change fewer rows per write. A guard before version 37 holds a line of at most 64 KiB: the engine refuses a longer one with the same reason, and the message asks the person to upgrade the guard (`basalt upgrade`). No size of a held write's lines is ever `INTERNAL`.
- **The signing pages** (approval and revert). The page holds every value in full and hashes the lines as before. The pages API is not budgeted, and an item this large reaches a page only on a short link: the engine's local pages, or the portal over the Vault up to 15 MiB (above). It takes each value's text out of the line text exactly as given, never parsed and serialized again: the raw slice for a non-string, and for a string the slice with its JSON escapes decoded (its characters, as in the marker), so its hash matches the marker's. A value whose text is over 1 MiB (the page tests the text, not the budget's cost, on purpose: it may show a value an agent saw marked) is shown as "too large to show: <size>, sha256 <hash>" (the size in KiB or MiB with one decimal, the hash in full, as in the marker), with the line "Your signature covers the full value." The person who needs to see it reads it with the CLI (`basalt log`, `basalt revert --dry-run`), which is not budgeted.
- **The CLI** (`basalt log`, `basalt revert`, `basalt approve`) prints values in full: the budget and the markers are for agents only. In the text output, a value that costs over 1 MiB as the marker counts it (so every value an agent sees marked) is followed by `(<size>, sha256 <hash>)`, so the person can match it to a marker; `--json` is unchanged.
- **`describe`** keeps a plain `SIZE_LIMIT` with a smaller `limit`. Its items are catalog data the person wrote, not ledger values; at `limit` 1 only a column or table comment of many megabytes could still be over, which the person wrote; the generic step stands.

### `describe`

M4 (owner decisions D-1 to D-7). The agent's map of the database. It reads only the catalog and statistics counters: never a row, never a sample value (no `pg_stats` most-common values or histograms). It takes no SQL and no filter on data, and it is not `EXPLAIN`. Every result carries `form`: `OVERVIEW`, `SEARCH` or `RESOURCE`. At most one of `query` and `resource` may be given; both is `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, as is a malformed `resource`, a `query` outside 1 to 100 characters, or a cursor that no longer matches.

- **`OVERVIEW`** (`describe()`): `store` (`{ kind: POSTGRES, database, host }`: the name and host of this agent's own database, never a user, port, password or credential, and never another agent's database), `fingerprint` (the catalog fingerprint), `counts` (`schemas` and `tables` the agent can see, tables including views, materialized views and partitioned parents, and `notGranted`, the tables in user schemas it cannot read), `limits` (`readRowLimit`, `statementTimeoutMs` and `writePolicy`: up to 10 rows allowed, 11 to 50 held for approval, over 50 refused, every `DELETE` held, cascaded rows counted). Up to 50 visible tables: `resources`, one line per table (the search item below). Over 50: `schemas`, one line per schema with its table count, and the result case `OVERVIEW_LARGE` (a `CALL_TOOL` step: search). `schemas` lists at most 50 schemas, the ones with the most visible tables first (ties by schema name), so the answer stays small whatever the number of schemas (D-3, one schema per tenant); when more than 50 schemas are visible, `otherSchemas` (`{ schemas, tables }`) counts the schemas left out and the visible tables in them, is absent otherwise, and the `OVERVIEW_LARGE` step's `describe` query (the largest schema) is how the agent reaches them: it searches by name. `counts.schemas` is always the full number. With `notGranted` above 0: the result case `NOT_GRANTED`, a `personMessage` with the count and `basalt access`, and no `ASK_PERSON` step, because platform schemas (Supabase's `auth`, `storage`, `realtime`) are ungranted on every project.
- **`SEARCH`** (`describe(query, limit?, cursor?)`): `query` matches case-insensitively against schema, table and column names and table comments; it is a name filter, never SQL. Ranked: exact table name, table name prefix, table name contains, schema name contains, column name, comment; ties by `schema.table`. `items` are `{ resource, kind, estimatedRows, matched, access, comment }`, `matched` one of `table`, `schema`, `column <name>` or `comment`, `comment` at most 120 characters. `limit` default 50, at most 200; cursor-paged with `nextCursor`, like every list. A hidden column's name never matches.
- **`RESOURCE`** (`describe(resource, ifFingerprint?)`): `resource` is `schema.table`, quoted identifiers allowed. Fields, with the catalog each comes from: `kind` (`pg_class`: `TABLE`, `VIEW`, `MATERIALIZED_VIEW`, `PARTITIONED_TABLE`; partitions are folded under their parent); `comment` (`obj_description`); `fields` in column order, each `name`, `type` (`format_type`), `nullable`, `default` (the expression text), `generated`, `comment` (`col_description`) and `pii` (`class` and `entityType` from `basalt_meta.agent_pii_columns`, `UNCLASSIFIED` until M5.1 classifies it); `primaryKey`; `indexes` (`name`, `definition` from `pg_get_indexdef`, `unique`, `partial`, `sizeBytes`, `scans` from `pg_stat_user_indexes.idx_scan`); `foreignKeys`, outgoing and incoming (`direction`, `columns`, the other `table`, `onDelete`, `onUpdate`, from `pg_constraint`); `stats` (`estimatedRows` from `pg_class.reltuples`, `null` when never analyzed; `tableBytes`, `totalBytes`, `seqScans`, `indexScans`, `lastAnalyzed` from `pg_stat_user_tables`); `rls` (row-level security is on, so rows may be filtered); `actions` (what the agent may do, from its grants); `policy` (the write policy as it applies here, only on a guarded table the agent can write, by `INSERT`, `UPDATE` or `DELETE`; `null` on a read-only table and on a table the agent can write through a grant made outside `basalt init`, which the guard does not police); `fingerprint`.
- **Hidden tables and columns (D-1, D-2).** A table the agent cannot `SELECT` is never named, only counted (`counts.notGranted`). A column the agent cannot `SELECT` (column grants) and a column classed `SECRET` are not listed in `fields`; `hiddenFields` counts them. A foreign key whose other table the agent cannot read is not listed; `hiddenForeignKeys` counts them, and `cascadesIntoHidden` counts the unreadable tables a delete here reaches through cascading keys, transitively. An index, primary key or foreign key that touches a hidden column is left out whole and counted (`hiddenIndexes`, `hiddenKeys`, `hiddenForeignKeys`). "Touches a hidden column" includes a column that appears only in an index expression or a partial-index `WHERE` predicate. A column whose default or generated expression names a hidden column (an expression counts the same way) has `default: null` and `expressionHidden: true`.
- **Not found.** A resource that does not exist and one the agent cannot read get the same answer: `NOT_FOUND`, with the steps in [Next steps per code](#next-steps-per-code). The agent cannot use the difference to probe for names. The person's command, `basalt access`, is always a `PERSON` step: it does not resolve through `OPERATOR` to an `AGENT` `RUN_COMMAND`, because its output is exactly the names the agent should not read.
- **Comments (D-4).** `comment` fields hold text written by database users. They are returned as data about the schema, never as instructions, and the tool description says so. At most 500 characters in the resource form, 120 in search items. When the PII proxy (M5.3) runs, comments pass through it like any other result.
- **Fingerprints.** The resource `fingerprint` is the sha256 of the canonical JSON of the resource answer without `stats` and without index `sizeBytes` and `scans` (row counts and scan counters change constantly and must not invalidate a cache), with the PII catalog fingerprint mixed in. The catalog `fingerprint` is the sha256 of one catalog read over every visible table, sorted (each table with its kind, the agent's `SELECT`, `INSERT`, `UPDATE` and `DELETE` rights and its comment, each column the agent can `SELECT` with its type and nullability, each constraint and index definition), plus `notGranted` and the PII catalog fingerprint: it changes on any structural change, grant or table comment and stays the same as rows change. The lines it hashes are never shown. With `ifFingerprint` equal to the current resource fingerprint the result is only `{ form, resource, fingerprint, unchanged: true }`.
- **Visibility is a context rule, not a boundary (D-7).** Hiding names (hidden tables, hidden columns, cascades) keeps them out of the agent's normal path. They are not secret from it: an agent can still find them through `query` on `pg_catalog`, or by running `basalt` on an agent-operated engine. No SQL-guard rule on catalog reads comes with this tool. The tool description says so. A listed column's `type` name or `default` expression may also name another object, a row type or a sequence for example, as Postgres prints it, whether or not the agent can read that object.
- **Recording.** `describe` reads the catalog and statistics only, so like `status`, `list_runs` and `list_pending` it is not recorded in the ledger.
- **Errors.** `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, `NOT_FOUND`, `QUERY_TIMEOUT` (the search ran past the engine's statement timeout), `UNAVAILABLE`, `INTERNAL`. No new codes.

## CLI

`basalt init`, `basalt log --run`, `basalt approve`, `basalt revert` (first release), plus `basalt version`, from M4.1 `basalt passkey`, from M4 `basalt status`, `basalt upgrade` and `basalt access`, from M5.1 `basalt classify`, and `basalt portal verify`; planned, `basalt plugin` ([Plugins](#plugins)). Machine output with `--json` uses the wire format above. Paged output prints the next cursor on **stderr**. Exit codes: `0` ok, `1` error, `2` usage. Flag values a person types are lowercase kebab-case with an explicit default (`--agent-rls=ask|all-rows|none`), an input spelling only: anything stored, sent or written to the ledger uses the SCREAMING_SNAKE_CASE enum it maps to.

- `basalt init --agent NAME [--write schema.table,…] [--read schema.table,…]`: shows the setup SQL (agent password redacted), asks to confirm unless `--yes`, installs or upgrades `basalt_meta`, creates the agent role (an existing role keeps its password unless `--rotate-password`; the SQL sends Postgres only the password's SCRAM-SHA-256 verifier, computed by init, never the password, so a server log of the statement cannot reveal it; an existing role is taken over only when it is a plain agent, see below), attaches the guard (with the guard's row-level security policy on tables that have RLS enabled, version 8), registers the passkey, then sets up the engine. Before showing the SQL it finds the agent's tables where row-level security hides every row from the agent role and, with `--agent-rls=ask` (the default; the values are `ask`, `all-rows`, `none`) at a terminal, asks once per table `<schema.table> hides its rows with row-level security. Let <name> see all <n> rows of this table? [y/N]`; tables without RLS are never asked about. Yes adds the agent policy of version 11 to the SQL. `--agent-rls=all-rows` adds it for every such table without asking (the only way without a terminal or with `--yes`); `--agent-rls=none` skips the question. After installing, init warns for each agent table where RLS still hides every row (`basalt: warning: row-level security is on for ... and no policy lets <role> see rows; the agent will read 0 rows. ...`); `basalt log` and the engine log warn the same. At a terminal it asks once `Install the engine as a background service? ... [Y/n]` (`--yes` answers yes, `--no-service` no). Without a terminal and without either flag (answers piped in, for example), init stops before anything is written with a usage error (exit `2`) that names `--yes` and `--no-service`: it never falls back to same-user mode on its own. Yes: one `sudo basalt service install --config ... --user ... --agent NAME --os-user ... --credential-stdin` (the agent's connection string on stdin, never in arguments) creates the engine's OS user, writes the engine config (agent `name`, `osUser` and `store`: its `kind`, `POSTGRES`, and the `credentialFile` inside it) and the `0600` credential file (`<config dir>/agents/NAME.url`) owned by that user in the service's system directories, installs and starts (or restarts) the unit. Before any step it refuses a basalt binary that anyone but root could change (the file and every directory above it must be root-owned and not writable by group or others), and it reaches every directory it writes in from `/` without following a symlink anyone but root controls, refusing a directory on the way owned by anyone but root or the engine's user or writable by group or others, and an existing directory owned by anyone else; init then checks the engine answers MCP `initialize` on the agent's socket, retrying for up to 20 seconds while the service manager starts or restarts it (init has already closed the page server it ran for the passkey registration, so the engine's pages port is free), and when it never answers, names the last error line of the engine's log it can read (`/Library/Logs/Basalt/engine.log` on macOS, the unit's journal on Linux) with the log's location; it then prints the `basalt mcp --agent NAME` line. No, or any of `--config`, `--socket-dir`, `--credential-file` given: init writes the config and credential itself (under the person's config directory when the paths are the system defaults) and prints the one `basalt engine --config ...` command. With the `claude` CLI on `PATH`, at a terminal, it offers `claude mcp add --scope local basalt-NAME -- basalt mcp --agent NAME` and runs it only on yes. `--scope local` is always passed, and init prints `basalt: the server basalt-NAME is registered for this project directory only (--scope local); sessions in other projects do not see it`: one MCP server serves one agent and so one database, and the registration decides which database a session reaches. [MCP clients](docs/mcp-clients.md) shows the same per-project setup for other clients. The admin connection string comes from `BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL` or a hidden prompt and is never written anywhere. It must be a `postgres://` or `postgresql://` URL; a keyword/value string (`host=... user=...`), or a URL that does not parse (a Unix socket directory goes in `?host=`, not percent-encoded in the host), is refused before init connects. The agent's connection string is built from it: its scheme, hosts, ports and database, the agent's user (`<prefix>_agent_<name>`, or `<prefix>_agent_<name>.<ref>` through Supabase's pooler) and password, and only these parameters: `host`, `port`, `dbname`, `sslmode`, `sslrootcert`, `sslcrl`, `sslcertmode`, `sslnegotiation`, `sslsni`, `channel_binding`, `require_auth`, `target_session_attrs`, `connect_timeout`, `application_name`, `min_protocol_version`, `max_protocol_version` and `default_query_exec_mode`. Every other parameter is dropped, among them `user`, `password`, `passfile`, `service` and `servicefile`, and the admin's TLS client identity `sslcert`, `sslkey` and `sslpassword` (CO-9). `sslrootcert` and `sslcrl` are file paths: after `service install` the engine's OS user must be able to read them. `--dry-run` prints the SQL and the engine setup (the sudo command and its steps) and runs nothing. M5.1: after installing, init prints `basalt: N columns the agent can read are not classified yet; run basalt classify` when there are any ([PII classification](#engine-basalt-log-and-basalt-init)).
- **Plain agent roles only.** Whenever init gives a role `<prefix>_agent_<name>` that already exists `LOGIN`, a password or `<prefix>_agents` (keeping its password, `--rotate-password`, or an `AGENT` setup code), the same SQL first refuses, with `BS008` (`LEDGER_INPUT_INVALID`) and nothing changed, a role that is not a plain agent: one that has `SUPERUSER`, `CREATEROLE`, `CREATEDB`, `REPLICATION` or `BYPASSRLS`; that is a member (directly or not, inheriting or not) of any role other than `<prefix>_agents` (an allow-list: predefined roles such as `pg_read_all_data`, `pg_monitor` or `pg_signal_backend`, `<prefix>_guard`, and any other group are all refused); that owns, or is a member of the owner of, the database or any object in it (`pg_shdepend`; the owner of a function, view or default a guarded write runs could replace it and run code as the next writer); or that holds a direct privilege on anything in `basalt_meta`, or `TRUNCATE`, `TRIGGER`, `REFERENCES` or `MAINTAIN` on an agent or guarded table. It then resets the role's own settings (`ALTER ROLE ... RESET ALL`, everywhere and `IN DATABASE` this one) before setting `statement_timeout` and `CONNECTION LIMIT 8`, since a setting such as `session_replication_role = replica` would keep the guard's triggers from firing; a setting the admin may not reset (one only a superuser may set, when the admin is not one) is refused. An `AGENT` setup code also refuses a role outside `<prefix>_agents`.
- `basalt init --demo` (the docs quickstart): `init` for a sample database, in one command. It is the same init (admin connection string, the setup SQL shown and confirmed, guard, passkey, engine), with these differences.
  - **What it creates.** The schema `basalt_demo` with `leads` (30 rows) and `lead_notes` (20 rows, two each on leads 1 to 10, `ON DELETE CASCADE` from `leads`), with row-level security off, from SQL embedded in the binary. It creates the agent `demo_bot` (`--agent` renames it) with `--write basalt_demo.leads,basalt_demo.lead_notes` and nothing else; `--write` and `--read` with `--demo` are a usage error (exit `2`). `USAGE` on `basalt_demo` and the agent's grants are the only access it gets; no other schema gets a table, a change or a grant. Apart from `basalt_demo`, init adds only what it always adds: `basalt_meta`, the roles and the engine setup. The `--agent-rls` check finds nothing to ask about, because row-level security is off on the demo tables.
  - **One confirmation, nothing kept before yes.** The sample-data SQL is shown first, followed by the setup SQL, and `Run this SQL as <user>?` is asked once for both. To plan the guard init needs the sample tables to exist, so it creates them in a transaction on the admin connection, plans, and rolls that transaction back before it shows the SQL and asks: nothing is committed, and nothing is held open, while the person decides. On yes, it creates the sample data again and applies the install in one fresh transaction that commits once, so a change made meanwhile is caught by the check that every agent-writable table is guarded. `--dry-run` also needs the admin role to be allowed to create in the database, because it plans the same way. `--yes`, `--skip-passkey`, `--no-service` and the other init flags work as for init.
  - **Safe to run again.** When `basalt_demo.leads` and `basalt_demo.lead_notes` both exist, init leaves their rows as they are (it never reseeds over a person's edits), prints `basalt: sample schema basalt_demo already exists; its rows are left as they are`, and installs and guards as usual. When only one exists, init creates the other with its rows, its index and row-level security off, and does not touch the one that exists (no row, index or row-level security change); when the existing `leads` does not have the ids or the columns `lead_notes` needs, the statement fails with the database's error and the transaction rolls back. When a guarded table of the demo is missing from an init run without `--demo` (`--write basalt_demo.leads`), the error suggests `basalt init --demo`. A re-run keeps the agent's password as init does.
  - **The comment.** Init sets the schema comment `BasaltDB demo data` only when it creates the schema in that run, never on a `basalt_demo` that already exists (one from `examples/demo/seed.sql` or made by hand).
  - **Output.** After the usual `basalt: guard installed; agent role <role>` and passkey lines, one line: `basalt: demo ready: leads 30, lead_notes 20` (the counts are read from the tables, so a re-run shows the current ones).
  - **Saved flags.** `--demo` is a one-shot flag and is never saved: `initArgs` in `engine.json` holds `--agent` and `--write` like any init. Running init again later does not reseed, and after `basalt_demo` is gone it fails on the saved `--write` (the table is missing) until `--demo` is given again.
  - **Removal.** `basalt uninstall` removes `basalt_demo` before it drops `basalt_meta` and the roles, in the same transaction, only when the schema carries the comment `BasaltDB demo data`. It runs `DROP TABLE basalt_demo.lead_notes`, `DROP TABLE basalt_demo.leads` and `DROP SCHEMA basalt_demo`, never with `CASCADE`, so it cannot drop a person's view, foreign key or table that depends on or sits in the schema. When one of those statements fails (something outside depends on the tables, or the schema holds other objects), uninstall skips the demo removal, rolling back only those three statements, prints `basalt: demo schema basalt_demo kept: <the database's message and detail>`, and still removes everything else. A `basalt_demo` without the comment is not touched. On success it prints `basalt: demo schema basalt_demo dropped`. The confirmation names the schema: `Drop basalt_meta, the ledger, every <prefix>_* role and the demo schema basalt_demo, if init --demo made it?`; `--yes` skips it.
- **Replication role default.** `basalt init`, `basalt upgrade` and a setup code read the default of `session_replication_role` a new session in the database gets: `ALTER DATABASE ... SET` first, then `ALTER ROLE ALL SET`, then the server's (`postgresql.conf`, `ALTER SYSTEM`, the command line; not read when the admin's own role settings hide it). When it is not `origin` they print, before the SQL runs, `basalt: warning: the database default of session_replication_role is <value>, not origin. ...` on stderr with the statement that fixes it (`ALTER DATABASE <db> RESET session_replication_role;`, `ALTER ROLE ALL RESET session_replication_role;`, or `ALTER SYSTEM RESET session_replication_role; SELECT pg_reload_conf();`), and go on. A warning, not a refusal: from version 26 the guard's triggers fire in any mode, so only foreign keys and the person's own triggers stop in agent sessions, and refusing would also block the upgrade that brings version 26.
- `basalt log [--run ID] [--full] [--sort -id|id]`: the agent's change records, newest first (`--sort` defaults to `-id`; `id` is oldest first; any other value is a usage error), read with the agent credential (`--credential-file`, then `--agent NAME` looked up in the engine config, then `BASALT_DATABASE_URL`; a flag always wins over the environment, and `basalt approve`, `basalt revert` and `basalt passkey` pick the credential the same way). Run as root, these commands, `basalt status` and `basalt upgrade` read a credential file only when it is owned by the owner of its directory (for the config's agent, the config directory's owner; root when root owns it), and from one a user other than root owns, including one root's own `basalt init --remote` wrote and handed to that user, they take only the strings root's `basalt init --remote` takes from the owner of `agents/` (step 8 of [`basalt init --remote`](#basalt-init---remote-the-engines-machine); exit `1` otherwise, never echoing the string, with `run the command as that user (sudo -u <user>), or fix the file`). Text output is compact (M4.1): one line per change; with `--run`, each row it wrote below it as its key and only the columns that changed, `{"id":4} status NEW -> CONTACTED` (an insert or a delete shows the key only), keeping the `(cascaded)`, `(read at xmin:N)` and `(blind write)` markers and `values hidden` or `values pruned`. Consecutive reads (`SELECT` records) collapse into one line: how many, the rows they returned and their tables (`3 reads, 42 rows: public.leads (2), public.notes (1)`). `--full` prints whole before and after rows and every read on its own line, as before M4.1. `--json` is unchanged. Warnings on stderr: broken ledger chain, unguarded tables, no engine heartbeat for 7 days, the last `DEAD_MAN` row (the engine came back after more than 7 days of silence), and from M4.1 no active passkey, and each passkey not used to sign an approval or revert for 90 days or more (counted from registration when never used), naming its label and id with `basalt passkey remove` as the fix for a lost one. The chain is checked incrementally with `verify_chain_from` from the head this user's `basalt log` last verified (kept under the user cache directory, per database and role; from [version 36](#version-36-reinstalls-on-the-record) with the guard install it was verified on, and a pin of an earlier install is reported with `record_pin_set_aside` and set aside, never silently, or, when it still verifies there, moved to the new install with a warning on stderr); from version 36 it prints the [reinstall notice](#version-36-reinstalls-on-the-record) on stderr for every reinstall the ledger records; `--full-check` runs `verify_chain()` from the anchor instead. M5.1: with a guard of version 15 or later it also prints the PII line `PII: N columns classified, M unclassified; dictionary of K values, last scan <time>` on stderr, and warns about unclassified columns and columns that need a review ([PII classification](#engine-basalt-log-and-basalt-init)).
- `basalt access --agent NAME [--config PATH] [--json]` (M4): read-only. Lists for one agent the tables it can read, the tables it can write, and the tables in user schemas it cannot read, each with the `basalt init` flag that would grant it (`--read schema.table` or `--write schema.table`, the same rebuild init does). Names print as Postgres prints them (`quote_ident`), as `describe` names them; a name that needs quotes is marked `grant by hand`. Each printed command is the saved flags plus one table, so two of them do not stack; under the not-granted list the output says to list several tables in one command or to run `basalt access` again after each grant. User schemas exclude `pg_catalog`, `information_schema`, `pg_toast*`, `pg_temp*`, `basalt_meta` and the schemas of installed extensions; Supabase's own schemas count like any other and are listed under their schema. Without a service it reads with the agent credential, as `basalt log` does; in service mode it uses the person socket method `LIST_ACCESS`. It prints names the `describe` tool only counts, so it is the person's command: the `describe` next steps name it as a `PERSON` step and never as an `AGENT` `RUN_COMMAND`, even on an agent-operated engine.
- `basalt crash list | show <id> | report <id>`: the crash reports basalt saved on this machine, and the GitHub issue link for one ([Crash reports](#crash-reports)). Nothing is sent. Under an agent, `report` opens and prints no link.
- `basalt portal verify [--portal-url URL] [--manifest PATH] [--json]` (vault channel VC-8, VC-22): read-only, needs no Postgres, no config and no engine. Fetches every file the binary embeds (`web.Files`: the four page directories, `lib/*.js`, `style.css`, the fonts and their license) from the portal origin and compares the bytes (SHA-256); a page `<dir>/index.html` is fetched at `/<dir>/`. The list comes from the embed, so a new embedded file is checked without a code change. It also fetches each route people load a page at (`internal/engine/pages` `PageRoutes`: `/approvals/<id>`, `/reverts/<id>`, `/passkeys/register`, `/passkeys/check`, `/passkeys/forget`, with a fixed sample id), which the portal's `_redirects` rewrites to the page files, and compares each with its page file: a rewrite to another page is seen. One line per path, `STATUS  path`, then a summary: `OK`; `DIFFERS` (served bytes differ); `MISSING` (not served); `HEADER` (right bytes, but the response's `Content-Security-Policy` differs from the engine's `internal/websec` policy or is absent, or a `.js` file is not `text/javascript`; the detail names the header); `ERROR` (could not be checked: network, an HTTP status other than 200 or 404 for an embedded file or a route, a redirect to another origin); `UNEXPECTED` (a path the portal build never publishes answers 200: `embed.go`, `embed_test.go`, `lib/<name>.test.mjs` for each embedded script; a static host has no listing, so only these named paths are probed; for these probes only, 403, 404 and 410 count as not served, and any other status except 200 is `ERROR`). Redirects within the portal's origin (same scheme, host and port) are followed (at most 5), any other redirect is `ERROR` and is not followed. With `--manifest PATH` it also checks the embedded files against that release manifest, a plain `sha256  path` list as in the committed `web/portal.sha256` (sorted by path, written by `make web-sri`, not embedded and not served), and prints `release manifest (PATH)` as `OK` or `DIFFERS` with the paths that differ; without the flag there is no manifest line, since the binary would only be checking itself. `--portal-url` defaults to the compiled-in portal origin `https://portal.basaltvault.app` and takes only an `https://` origin, or an `http://` origin on a loopback host (`localhost`, `127.0.0.1`, `::1`: tests); with a path, query, credentials, any other scheme or any other `http://` host it is a usage error. A `--manifest` file that cannot be read or parsed (bad line, duplicate path) is a usage error. Exit `0` when every path and the manifest are `OK`, `1` when anything else, `2` for usage errors. `--json` prints `{ "portalUrl", "ok", "checked", "files": [{ "path", "status", "detail" }], "manifest": { "source", "status", "mismatches" } }` (`manifest` only with `--manifest`), [`portal-verify-result.schema.json`](api/mcp/portal/portal-verify-result.schema.json). **Daily check.** The engine runs the same comparison (the embedded files, the routes, the headers; no `--manifest`) against the portal in its config: at start and then every 24 hours, only when `pagesUrl` is `https://portal.basaltvault.app` (with local pages it runs nothing). It reads public pages and sends nothing about the engine. It keeps the last result in memory and in `<stateDir>/portal-check.json` (`0600`; the portal checked, the time, the paths with `DIFFERS`, `MISSING`, `HEADER` or `UNEXPECTED`, at most 100, and the counts). When that result has any such path, `status` (the tool, and `basalt status`, which reads the file when the engine runs as the person) reports the problem `PORTAL_MISMATCH` (`WARNING`, [Next steps](#next-steps)): `message` names the counts and the day of the check, and the one step is a `PERSON` `RUN_COMMAND` `basalt portal verify` to see the files. `ERROR` paths and a check that could not run are only logged and give no problem. The warning does not stop any tool. **Releases.** Each release attaches `portal.sha256`, the `web/portal.sha256` of the tagged commit, for `--manifest`. Limit: it catches a portal that serves different files to everyone, not one that serves the checker something else than a browser (it sends a plain client's request), not a page served to one person only, and not a compromise of this binary's own download.
- `basalt classify [--review=changed|all] [--set schema.table.column=class[:entity-type]]... [--phone-region=auto|XX] [--yes] [--dry-run] [--json] [--check]` (M5.1): suggests a class for every column the agents can read and records the classes the person confirms, with the admin connection string given as for init. Every flag and its default: [`basalt classify`](#basalt-classify).
- Engine config (`engine.json`) also takes `stateDir` (default `/var/lib/basalt` on Linux, the systemd `StateDirectory`; `/Library/Application Support/Basalt/state` on macOS): the engine keeps each agent's last verified chain head there (`<stateDir>/<agent>.head.json`, mode `0600`; from [version 36](#version-36-reinstalls-on-the-record) with `installId`, the guard install it was verified on, and `setAside`, its kept copy of the pins it set aside) and verifies from it on start and every hour.
- **One engine, many agents.** One engine may serve many agents. `engine.json` lists them under `agents`, each with its own name, OS user, socket (`<socketDir>/<name>.sock`) and credential (`store.credentialFile`, the connection string of its own `<prefix>_agent_<name>` role). Those credentials may point at different databases on different hosts, or at the same one. The engine opens a separate connection pool for each agent from that agent's credential; agents never share a credential or a connection. An agent's ledger, grants, connections and status are scoped to that agent and its database: the change record and its chain live in that database's `basalt_meta` (the engine pins the agent's verified chain head in `<stateDir>/<agent>.head.json`), its grants are the ones init gave its role there, and the `status` tool on its socket and `basalt status --agent NAME` report that agent and its database only. The connection limit is per agent role, not an engine-wide pool: init sets `CONNECTION LIMIT 8` on each agent role and the engine's pool for each agent opens at most 4 of them, so an agent at its limit (`TOO_MANY_CONNECTIONS`) does not take connections from another agent on the same engine. Only the person's CLI lists every agent: `basalt status` without `--agent` shows each agent's name, database name and host (see AE-13); neither MCP tool, `status` or `describe`, ever names another agent or its database, and an agent that runs `basalt` (an [agent marker](#agents-and-the-admin-credential) is set) is not given the list either. Each agent uses exactly one database. One agent across several databases, and a run, read or revert that spans databases (through `dblink`, a foreign data wrapper or otherwise), are not available.
- AE-10, AE-12 (AE-11 is planned). **Init saves its flags.** `basalt init` writes the flags it was run with to `engine.json`, per agent, as `initArgs` (an array of strings): `--agent`, `--write`, `--read`, `--prefix`, `--statement-timeout`, `--socket-dir`, `--os-user`, `--engine-user`, `--credential-file` (a path, not a secret; AE-15), `--passkey-origin`, `--agent-rls`, `--operated-by` and `--remote` (planned, [Setup from your laptop](#setup-from-your-laptop)). Init saves a flag only when its value is not the default, except `--agent` and `--os-user`, as `--flag=value`; a value that is a connection URL or holds `password=` is refused before anything is written. The engine reads them to build its commands. Never the admin connection string (init still prompts for it, or reads `BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL`), never `--database-url`, and never the one-shot flags (`--yes`, `--dry-run`, `--no-service`, `--skip-passkey`, `--add-passkey`, `--passkey-label`, `--rotate-password`, `--new-code`). Every "run `basalt init` again" step uses exactly those flags plus the one change it is for, and `--config` by the [Next steps](#next-steps) rule. **Re-running init keeps the agent's password** when its role exists (the credential file is left as it is). If the credential file is missing everywhere the engine setup reads it (the system path and, when init may still run the engine in the foreground, the person's config directory), as after an init that stopped before writing it, init says so and asks `Set a new password for it and write the credential? [Y/n]`; yes (or `--yes`, or `--dry-run`, which only shows it) sets a new password and writes the credential; no, or no terminal without `--yes`, stops with nothing changed. A missing file at a path given with `--credential-file` is refused instead, since it is more likely a typo. `--rotate-password` sets a new one, prints the engine restart command, and only then do the steps say to restart the engine.
- AE-11. `basalt upgrade --agent NAME [--config PATH] [--yes] [--dry-run]`: applies only the `basalt_meta` migrations this build has and the database lacks. It never rotates the agent's password, adds no policies, registers no passkey, and needs no engine restart (the engine checks the version on each call). First, with the agent's own credential (or, in service mode, the engine's `STATUS` and `TARGET` over the [person socket](#the-person-socket)), it reads the guard and the database the credential connects to; before asking for anything it stops with exit `0` when the guard is current (`basalt: the guard is up to date (version N); nothing to upgrade`), and refuses (exit `1`) a guard that is not installed (that is `basalt init`'s job), a guard newer than this build, and an agent-operated engine (below). The admin connection string is then asked once (hidden prompt, or `BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL`) and never stored; the CLI refuses it, before connecting, when it names a different database than the agent's credential (host, port and database as pgx connects, or the Supabase project ref and database), and after connecting when the database has no guard, a newer one, or no role for the agent. It prints `This upgrades Basalt's guard from version <from> to <to>; no agent changes.`, the prefix and missing versions, and the SQL; `--dry-run` stops there; else `Run this? [y/N]` unless `--yes`. It runs the SQL in one transaction (with the same unguarded-table check as `basalt init`) and prints what it applied. With `operatedBy: AGENT` the upgrade goes through an `UPGRADE` [setup code](#the-setup-code) instead: `basalt init --remote` on the engine's machine, `basalt init --setup-code` on the person's own computer; `basalt upgrade` refuses there without asking for the admin connection string (CO-9).
- AE-13. `basalt status [--agent NAME] [--config PATH] [--json]`: the twin of the `status` tool, for the person. Prints the config path, whether the engine runs (pid, OS user), the socket and state directories, the pages URL and whether the engine serves it, `operatedBy`, and per agent: guard version against the build (up to date, upgrade available, upgrade required), passkey registered, and each problem with its `personMessage` and commands. Never prints a secret, and never reads or prints the credential file's contents; the credential file's path appears only as a saved `--credential-file` flag in an init command (AE-15). `--json` is the `status` tool's result plus `configPath`, `engine.pid`, `engine.osUser`, `socketDir`, `stateDir` (`pid` and `osUser` absent while the engine is not running), and `engine.unknown` (decided, owner decisions EV-22) when it cannot tell whether the engine runs. `--json` and the text report also carry the agent's `database` (`{ host, name }`, text `Database: NAME on HOST`): the database name and host its credential connects to, never a user, port, password, connection string or credential path. Without `--agent`, and with no agent marker set: an engine with one agent reports that agent; with several, `basalt status` prints a table of the engine's agents, one line each with the agent, its host and its database (`--json`: `{ "agents": [{ "agent", "host", "database" }], "configPath" }`, plus `problem` on an agent whose credential cannot be read, which then has no host or database), then the line `basalt status --agent NAME prints one agent's full report.` The table has no user, port, password, connection string, credential path or connection count, and implies no shared pool: each agent's connection limit is its own. With an agent marker set (the same detection as [Agents and the admin credential](#agents-and-the-admin-credential)) no other agent is listed or named: without `--agent` and with several agents it stays a usage error (exit `2`) that asks for `--agent NAME` and adds `The person can see every agent and its database by running basalt status in their own terminal.` In service mode it asks the engine (`STATUS` and `TARGET` for one agent, `AGENTS` for the table, over the [person socket](#the-person-socket)). In same-user mode it reads the config and the agent's credential itself, as the engine would: the engine runs when it holds its lock file `<socket dir>/engine.lock` (which holds its pid; its OS user is the file's owner), and while it does not, the report has `engine.reachable` false and the problem `ENGINE_UNREACHABLE` with the start command, as the shim answers. A lock file the CLI cannot open read-write (another OS user's engine) is `engine.unknown` true, `engine.reachable` false and no `ENGINE_UNREACHABLE` problem, never "not running". The probe takes a free lock for an instant, so an engine started in that instant stops with "already running" and is started again. Exit `0` whatever the problems.
- `basalt uninstall`: drops the guard's RLS policies, the demo schema `basalt_demo` when `basalt init --demo` made it (without `CASCADE`), `basalt_meta` and the roles, with the admin connection string given the same way as for init.
- `basalt approve <response-code>` (M2): decodes a `basalt-approval.1.` code, checks it locally against the stored passkey, and submits it with `submit_approval` using the agent credential (looked up as for `basalt log`). Without `--agent`, `--credential-file` or `BASALT_DATABASE_URL`, `basalt approve` with a code, `--show` or `--open` uses the only agent the engine serves (in service mode the agent sockets in the socket directory, else the agents in `engine.json`), or, when it serves several, the one whose ledger holds that approval; when none or more than one does, a usage error names the agents. `basalt revert --apply`, `--status` and `--reject` pick the agent the same way from the revert. `basalt approve --show <approvalId>` prints the approval link for a held action, after a headline on stderr that states its cascaded rows (owner decisions, Decided Nov 12). `basalt approve --open <approvalId>` does the same but opens the link in the default browser (`open` on macOS, `xdg-open` on Linux) instead of printing it; when no browser opens it prints the link on stdout. The links use the pages base URL `--pages-url`, else the engine config's `pagesUrl` when the agent is named with `--agent`, else the engine's local pages `http://localhost:8787` ([Page links](#page-links)). M4.1: when the engine serves the [local pages](#local-pages) (it answers `GET /healthz` on the config's pages address), `--show` and `--open` use a newly issued short link (revoking the item's earlier one, [Link secrets](#link-secrets); `--reissue` is accepted and means the same) and the page relays the signature itself, so no code is pasted. The CLI then waits, as `basalt revert` does: it polls the approval and ends with exit `0` when it is signed (`approval <id> signed; the agent applies it by repeating the write`) or already applied, and with exit `1` when it expired unsigned. A pasted code is still taken while it waits. When the engine does not serve the pages, or issues no short link, they use the long link, whose page shows a code to paste, and do not wait; `basalt approve '<code>'` stays for a code from the long-link page. The CLI is only a relay; Postgres checks the signature.
- `basalt revert --change ID | --run ID | --task ID [--intent TEXT] [--dry-run] [--json]` (M3): prepares the revert with `prepare_revert` using the agent credential (looked up as for `basalt log`), prints every line on stdout (restored rows with the columns that change back, then skipped rows with the reason), prints the revert link on stderr, reads the pasted `basalt-revert.1.` code, checks it locally, and calls `apply_revert`. Without a code it exits `1` and nothing is reverted. Prints rows restored and rows skipped with reasons; exit `0` even when some rows were skipped. `--dry-run` prints the preview only (`preview_revert`). `basalt revert --apply <code>` applies a prepared revert later, within its hour. `--intent` defaults to `Revert <scope> <id>`. `--open` opens the revert link in the default browser instead of printing it (printed on stderr when no browser opens). The revert link uses the same pages base URL as `basalt approve` (`--pages-url`, the config's `pagesUrl`, else `http://localhost:8787`). M4.1: when the engine serves the local pages, the CLI prints (or with `--open` opens) the short link `<base>/reverts/<revertId>` and waits for the page's relay to apply the revert, polling its status, while still accepting a pasted code; it prints the same result either way. `preview_revert`'s `command` gains `--open` (and `--config` as for `approveCommand`). The CLI is only a relay; Postgres checks the signature and runs the revert. For a large plan (over 1000 lines) it prints the plan size and the lines the link carries instead of every line, and the code signs the plan: the agent then applies it in batches (`apply_revert_batch`), and `basalt revert --status <revertId>` prints its progress (restored, skipped, pending, batches, `applyBy`) and the lines skipped so far. After signing a large plan it prints `basalt: tell your agent: apply revert <revertId> in batches` (the person's next step, AE-5) before the `--status` hint. Planned (revert scope, RS-2, [version 20](#version-20-revert-scope)): `--change`, `--run` and `--task` prepare only the `--agent` credential's own work (`--task`: its own lines, stored even when empty) and open the revert page, where the person widens; the CLI never widens by itself. `basalt revert --reject <revertId> [--note TEXT]` (served) rejects a pending revert as the page's Reject does. While the CLI waits for the page, a revert rejected there ends the wait with `the revert was rejected on the revert page; nothing was reverted` (exit `1`).

  **Scope flags** (served, revert scope, RS-2 and Q11). They narrow what the CLI prepares inside the credential's own work, the same filters the agent's `preview_revert` and `request_revert` take, and they work with `--dry-run`:
  - `--runs ID,ID` (only with `--task`): 1 to 20 of the credential's own runs with that task id. `--tables schema.table,…` (with any scope): 1 to 20 guarded tables the credential wrote in the scope. Postgres checks both ([version 20](#version-20-revert-scope)); a refused entry is printed with the same fixed text as the agent's `SCOPE_FILTER`, so the flags cannot probe which runs or tables exist. More than 20 entries, `--runs` without `--task`, or an entry that is not a uuid or a `schema.table` name is a usage error (exit `2`).
  - `--agents NAME,NAME|all` (default: the `--agent` credential's own agent). Naming only that agent is the default. Anything wider (`all`, or another agent) needs the person's widen proof, which only the revert page makes: the CLI prepares the credential's own work as without the flag, then opens the revert page (as `--open` does) and prints `basalt: choose "Include other agents' changes" on the revert page to add other agents' changes`. When the engine does not serve the local pages to this CLI (it answers no `GET /healthz` there), `--agents` wider than the credential is a usage error: widening needs the page from the engine, and the long link the CLI prints otherwise cannot widen. Agent names are checked by Postgres on the page, not by the CLI.
  - `--rescope <revertId>`: changes the scope of a pending, unsigned revert the credential prepared (an agent's request included) instead of preparing a new one, so the agent's wait sees the result. It takes `--runs`, `--tables` and `--agents` as above and keeps the revert's scope kind and id; with no filter it goes back to the credential's whole own work in that scope. It calls `rescope_revert` with no proof or token, so it is refused for a select outside the credential's own work and for a revert the person widened; the refusal says `cannot change the scope of revert <id>`, with Postgres's detail, and that a revert the person widened is changed on the revert page. On success it prints the lines and the link, opens it with `--open`, and waits or reads the code, as for a new revert. `--rescope` with `--change`, `--run`, `--task`, `--apply`, `--status`, `--reject` or `--dry-run` is a usage error.
  - `--reject <revertId> [--note TEXT]`: calls `reject_revert` with the credential (no token, so it is refused for a revert the person widened: only the page holding the rotated link can reject it) and prints `basalt: revert <id> rejected; nothing was reverted` with the time. The agent's wait returns `REJECTED`. The note is at most 500 characters; the agent reads it. A signed, expired or already rejected revert is refused with Postgres's detail (exit `1`). `--note` without `--reject`, or `--reject` with any other revert flag but `--agent`, `--config`, `--credential-file` and `--json`, is a usage error. `--json` prints `{ "revertId", "status": "REJECTED", "rejectedAt" }`.
- `basalt init` (M2) registers the person's passkey: it prints and opens a registration link, reads the pasted `basalt-passkey.1.` code, verifies it, and stores the public key with the admin credential, unless an active passkey is registered already for the origin `--passkey-origin` picks (a passkey signs only on its own origin, so an engine handed over to an agent with `--operated-by=agent` gets a portal passkey even when a local one exists, as `--setup-code` does). `--skip-passkey` defers it, `--add-passkey` registers another, `--passkey-label` names it (default `<database> · <agent> · <date>`, the date local, at most 64 bytes, the database name shortened first, so the picker tells this database's passkey from the person's others on the same site), and `--passkey-origin` picks the pages the passkey belongs to: the engine's local pages `http://localhost:<port>` (default `http://localhost:8787`, RP ID `localhost`) or the portal `https://portal.basaltvault.app` (RP ID `basaltvault.app`); any other origin is a usage error ([Page origins](#page-origins)). Init writes it to the engine config as `pagesUrl`. M4.1: for a local origin init serves the built-in pages on that port (127.0.0.1) while it waits for the code, unless the port is taken. When init serves the pages itself, the page hands the passkey back to init and the person types a short pairing code instead of pasting the long one: the registration request carries `handBack`, a secret of 16 random bytes (base64url, [`registration-request.schema.json`](api/mcp/approval/registration-request.schema.json)) in the link's fragment, and after the passkey is created the page posts the registration response JSON (the JSON the `basalt-passkey.1.` code carries) to `POST /api/v1/passkeys/register` on that server, with the secret in the `basalt-link-secret` header. Init checks, in order: `Origin` equal to the page origin (`403`), the secret, compared as `sha256` in constant time (`404` for a missing or wrong secret), fewer than 4 registrations kept (`429` `RATE_LIMITED`, `basalt init received more passkeys than it takes`; nothing changes, and the count is checked again before a verified one is kept), `Content-Type: application/json` (`415`), at most 16 KiB (`413`), a registration response that verifies against the request (`400`). Init reads and verifies a body without holding up other posts, and its page server bounds a whole request at 20 seconds, so a post that stalls its body blocks neither another post nor the prompt; errors are `{ "code", "message" }` as on the [Local pages](#local-pages). A response that verifies is kept (the same registration posted again is kept once) and init answers `200 { "status": "RECEIVED" }`; it stores nothing yet. **The threat:** the link is visible to other programs on the machine (the terminal, the browser's command line), so one running as any local user, the agent included, could post a key pair of its own first and, if init stored it, approve its own held writes. So only after the `200` the page shows the [pairing code](#codes) of the passkey it created (`XXXX-XXXX`, 8 Crockford base32 characters from the request's challenge, the credential id and the public key) with `Type or paste this code into basalt init`, and init asks `basalt: Type or paste the code the page shows:`. Init stores only the kept registration whose pairing code the person enters (typed in any case, with or without the dash or spaces). Each other entry counts as a try: one that is not 8 Crockford characters gets `basalt: that is not the code the page shows (8 letters and digits, like K7Q2-9MX4); check the page`, one that matches no kept registration `basalt: that code doesn't match; check the page`, and one that matches more than one `basalt: that code matches more than one passkey sent to basalt init, so it cannot tell which is yours; check the page`. After 3 tries init fails with `run basalt init again` and stores nothing. A paste that cannot be read (over 64 KiB, a read error) is reported and does not use a try; after 10 such pastes init fails without a passkey. When 4 registrations are kept, init says once `basalt: basalt init received more passkeys than expected; if the page shows no pairing code, paste the long code from it`. A `basalt-passkey.1.` code pasted at the same prompt registers as without a hand-back. A closed stdin ends init without a passkey: the post alone never registers one. When the post fails because init is no longer there (no answer, or `404`), the page says `basalt init is no longer waiting; run it again` and shows no code; on any other refusal it shows the error and the long code to paste. Init adds `handBack` only to links of the pages it serves (never to the portal's, or when the port is taken), and the route exists only on init's server, never on the engine's listener.
- **Pasted codes.** Every prompt that reads a pasted code (`basalt init`'s passkey code or pairing code, `basalt approve` and `basalt revert` without a code argument, including while `--open` waits for the page, `basalt passkey check`) reads it at a terminal with canonical mode and echo off, echoing it and handling backspace itself, and restores the terminal's modes when it returns: a canonical-mode line holds only 1024 bytes on macOS (`MAX_CANON`; 4096 on Linux), and the codes are longer. Enter ends the code, Ctrl-D on an empty line ends the input, Ctrl-C stops the command; surrounding space is trimmed; a code is at most 64 KiB. Without a terminal, or on a platform where basalt does not set the terminal's modes (other than macOS and Linux), the code is one line of stdin. While `basalt approve --open`/`--show` or `basalt revert` also waits for the page, a paste that cannot be read (over 64 KiB, a read error) is reported on stderr and the wait goes on for the page.
- **Revert line order** (M4.1).
