The text output of `basalt revert` (the lines, `--dry-run`, and the skipped lines of `--status`) is ordered for reading: rows to restore first, re-inserts before restored updates before deletes, then skipped rows; inside each group by table, then by primary key in natural order (numbers by value, text with digit runs compared by value: `1, 3, 5, 10`, never `1, 10, 3`), redacted lines last in their table by row hash. Only the display changes: `--json`, the link, the binding and `rowsHash` keep the signed order.
- `basalt passkey list [--json]` (M4.1): the database's active passkeys, read with the agent credential (looked up as for `basalt log`; in service mode over the [person socket](#the-person-socket), `LIST_PASSKEYS`): credential id (base64url), label, RP ID, when registered and when last used (`never`). `--json` prints `[{ "credentialId", "label", "rpId", "origin", "createdAt", "lastUsedAt" }]` (`lastUsedAt` null when never used). Never prints key material. At most 100 passkeys (the bound the relays use), so it has no cursor.
- `basalt passkey remove <credentialId> [--force] [--yes] [--pages-url URL] [--open]` (M4.1): revokes a passkey in Postgres with the admin connection string (`BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL` or a hidden prompt, as for init). The id is the full base64url id or a unique prefix of at least 8 characters. An id that starts with `-` (base64url has that character) is accepted as it is, and `--` before it also works. It shows the label, RP ID and last use and asks `Remove passkey ...? [y/N]` unless `--yes`. The last active passkey is refused unless `--force` is given and the person types its label at the prompt (asked even with `--yes`); held writes and reverts then cannot be signed until `basalt init --add-passkey`. It calls `revoke_passkey`, which records a `PASSKEY_REVOKED` ledger row. Revoking does not touch the device: it then prints the forget link (`<base>/passkeys/forget#1.<request>`, mode `FORGET`, [Passkey management](#passkey-management)) on stdout, to open on each device that holds the passkey so its password manager drops it; `--open` opens it in the default browser instead (printed when no browser opens). `<base>` is `--pages-url`, else the passkey's registered origin; a base other than that origin gets no link, since only a page on the passkey's origin can signal about it.
- `basalt passkey check [--pages-url URL] [--open]` (M4.1): which registered passkeys this device can still produce. Reads the active passkeys with the agent credential (looked up as for `basalt log`; in service mode over the person socket, `LIST_PASSKEYS`), prints the check link (`<base>/passkeys/check#1.<request>`, mode `CHECK`; `--open` opens it as for `basalt approve --open`) for the passkeys registered on the pages base (`--pages-url`, else the engine config's `pagesUrl` (in service mode the engine's, from `PAGES`), else `http://localhost:8787`) and names any registered on another origin with the `--pages-url` that checks them; when the base is local and the engine is not serving it, the CLI serves the pages there while it waits, as `basalt init` does for registration. It reads the pasted `basalt-passkey-check.1.` code, verifies each assertion in it locally against the stored public key and the check challenge, and prints each passkey as `found on this device` or `not found on this device`. For each one not found it asks `Remove <label> (<id>)? [y/N]`; yes runs the `basalt passkey remove` steps (admin connection string asked once). Nothing is written unless a removal is confirmed. A check signature authorizes nothing (see [Passkey management](#passkey-management)).
- `basalt engine list [--admin] [--json]` (M4.1; [Direct transport](#direct-transport)): the engines recorded in the database ([Version 33](#version-33-engine-keys-and-the-pinned-address)), read through `agent_engines` with the agent credential (looked up as `basalt log` finds it outside service mode: `--agent`, `--credential-file` or `BASALT_DATABASE_URL`; an engine run as a service whose credential this user cannot read uses `--admin`), or with `--admin` through `engines` with the admin connection string (`BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL` or a hidden prompt), for the person's own computer, which has no agent credential. One line per engine: its fingerprint, address (`no address`), when recorded, and `revoked <time UTC>` when revoked; with the agent credential, the engine's own row is marked `this engine`. `--json` prints `[{ "engineId", "fingerprint", "url", "recordedAt", "revokedAt", "self" }]` (`url` and `revokedAt` null when absent, `self` false with `--admin`). Never more than 100 rows (the newest first); it has no cursor. A guard older than version 33 prints `this database records no engines (guard version <n>; basalt upgrade adds them)` and exits `0`.
- `basalt engine revoke [<engine>] [--yes]` (M4.1): revokes an engine with the admin connection string, as `basalt passkey remove` does a passkey. `<engine>` is the engine id, or a unique prefix of at least 8 characters of it or of its fingerprint (spaces ignored); without it, it lists the engines not revoked and asks which (`Which engine? Type the start of its fingerprint:`). It shows the fingerprint, address and when recorded, and asks `Revoke engine <fingerprint>? Its portal links stop working; approvals go on through long links. [y/N]` unless `--yes`. It calls `revoke_engine` (an `ENGINE_REVOKED` ledger row) and prints what the engine does next ([Direct transport](#direct-transport), Revocation by the person) and that each device should remove its pin (`https://portal.basaltvault.app/engines`). An unknown, ambiguous or already revoked engine is exit `1`, nothing changed. It never asks for or touches an engine config: it runs on the person's computer.
- `basalt tunnel [--address https://<host>[:<port>]] [--direct-addr <host:port>] [--config PATH]` (M4.1; [Direct transport](#direct-transport)): helps the person publish the engine's direct listener through their own tunnel. It prints the commands for Cloudflare Tunnel (`cloudflared tunnel create`, `route dns`, `run --url http://<directAddr>`), Tailscale Funnel (`tailscale funnel --bg http://<directAddr>`) and a Caddy reverse proxy (`reverse_proxy <directAddr>`), each forwarding to `directAddr` (`--direct-addr`, else `engine.json`, else `127.0.0.1:8787`, said on stderr when the engine does not have it yet) and each passing the public `Host` on unchanged. Without `--address` the commands use `engine.example.com`. With `--address` (checked by the [engine address grammar](#direct-transport); a refused one is exit `2`) the commands name that host and the command then fetches `<address>/v1/engine` with no `Origin`, no credentials and no redirect (10 seconds, at most 4 KiB), checks that `engineId` is the hash of `engineKey` and, when this machine's key file is readable, that it is this engine's, and prints the fingerprint with the next steps (`basalt init --setup-code <code> --engine-url <address>` on the person's computer; the pin page on each device). Exit `1`, with the reason, when the address does not answer, answers `400` with the body `host not allowed` (the direct listener does not record that Host: the address is not recorded yet, the tunnel does not pass the public `Host` on unchanged, or the recorded address has another port; the local pages' `host not allowed: <host>` is the wrong listener), a gateway error or Cloudflare's `530` (the tunnel is not connected to the listener), another status or a non-JSON body, an id that is not the key's hash, or another engine's key. It reads no database and no secret and writes nothing.
- `basalt init --direct-addr <host:port|off>` (also `basalt init --remote`): sets `directAddr` in `engine.json` ([Direct transport](#direct-transport)), with `--remote` or `--operated-by=agent` only (a usage error otherwise, and with a local `--passkey-origin`); a non-loopback address is a usage error (exit `2`). Saved in `initArgs`; a re-run without it keeps the saved value. The engine binds it at its next start.

- `basalt engine [--config PATH] [--user NAME] [--plugin-debug]`: runs the engine in the foreground, and restarts it onto a new `basalt` binary installed at the path it was started from ([Next steps](#next-steps), Restart onto an upgraded binary). **`--plugin-debug`** shows local plugins' stderr on the engine's own stderr; without it, and always for an engine run as a service (whose stderr is a log file), it is discarded ([Plugins, Failure](#failure)). **`--user NAME`** (containers without a service manager) makes the engine run as the OS user `NAME` before it opens the config, a credential, a socket or a port, in this order, stopping at the first failure with exit `1` and nothing read: (1) it resolves `NAME` (uid, gid, supplementary groups) and refuses a uid `0`, a gid `0` and membership of group `0`, and refuses unless it runs as root or already as `NAME` (`basalt engine --user NAME starts as root (a container entrypoint) or as NAME itself, not as uid N`); (2) it refuses its own binary when anyone but root could change it or a directory above it (the `basalt service install` check); (3) it sets the umask to `0077`; (4) started as root, it makes or takes over the socket directory and the state directory with the service's modes, owned by `NAME`, reached from `/` without following a symlink (Linux `/run/basalt` `0755` and `/var/lib/basalt` `0700`, the unit's `RuntimeDirectoryMode` and `StateDirectoryMode`; macOS `/Library/Application Support/Basalt/run` `0755` and `.../state` `0750`), then sets the supplementary groups, the gid and the uid, in that order, on every thread; (5) it sets `no_new_privs` on every thread (Linux; macOS has none, and a cgo build, which cannot set it on every thread, is refused); (6) it verifies the drop: on Linux every thread in `/proc/self/task` has all four uids and gids `NAME`'s, exactly its groups, no permitted, effective or ambient capability and `no_new_privs`; on macOS the real and effective ids and the groups; on both, `setuid(0)`, `seteuid(0)`, `setgid(0)` and `setegid(0)` must fail. It then prints `basalt engine: dropped root; running as NAME (uid U, gid G); no_new_privs set` (`already running as` when no drop was needed; no `no_new_privs` note on macOS) on stderr. A `socketDir` or `stateDir` in the config other than these defaults is not made before the drop: the engine, as `NAME`, handles it as it does without `--user`. Without systemd the unit's sandbox and memory limits do not apply: only the OS user split protects the credential. Started as root without `--user`, the engine reads its config as the root person CLI does: reached from `/` without following a symlink anyone but root controls, a regular file with one link owned by root or its directory's owner and not writable by group or others; anything else stops it with exit `1` before it starts. It then reaches each agent's files the way root's `basalt init --remote` does ([`basalt init --remote`](#basalt-init---remote-the-engines-machine), step 8): the credential file must be `agents/<agent>.url` next to the config (a `store.credentialFile` anywhere else stops that agent: `... as root, basalt engine uses only that path. Fix the agent's store.credentialFile in the config, or start the engine with --user`), and the config directory and `agents/` are opened once at start, from `/` without following a symlink anyone but root controls (every directory on the way owned by root or the config directory's owner, not writable by group or others); the credential file, its `.pending` and its `.lock` are then read, locked and renamed relative to `agents/`, and `engine.json` is locked, read and rewritten (when a setup is cleared) relative to the config directory, so a directory swapped for a symlink after the start redirects nothing. It reads a credential file or `.pending` that is a regular file with one link, mode `0600` or tighter, owned by root or by the owner of `agents/`, and from one the owner of `agents/` wrote it takes only what root's `basalt init --remote` takes from it (step 8: a `postgres://` or `postgresql://` URL with a password and no parameter that makes the driver read a file), else that agent stops (`... as root, basalt engine takes from it only a postgres:// or postgresql:// URL ...; start the engine with --user (as basalt service install does), or fix the file`), never echoing the string; a symlinked `.lock` is refused as a lock file that cannot be used.
- Service mode (owner decisions SM-1 to SM-3). **The person's CLI in service mode.** `basalt log`, `basalt approve`, `basalt revert` and `basalt passkey list` and `check` given `--agent NAME` and the default `--config` find out whether the engine runs as a background service by the installed unit, which the person can read: `/etc/systemd/system/basalt-engine.service` on Linux, `/Library/LaunchDaemons/dev.basaltdb.engine.plist` on macOS. Unit present: they go through the engine's [person socket](#the-person-socket) and never read `engine.json` or the credential file. The engine stopped (socket missing or refusing the connection): `basalt: the engine is not running; start it with <command>, then run this command again` and exit `1`, where `<command>` is `sudo systemctl start basalt-engine.service` on Linux or `sudo launchctl bootstrap system /Library/LaunchDaemons/dev.basaltdb.engine.plist; sudo launchctl kickstart -k system/dev.basaltdb.engine` on macOS. Never `permission denied`, never a fallback to the files. The engine refusing the person's uid: `basalt: the engine does not let OS user <uid> use its person socket; add it with sudo basalt init --agent NAME --person-uid <uid>` and exit `1`. No unit, a `--config` other than the default, `--credential-file` or `BASALT_DATABASE_URL`: same-user mode, the CLI reads the credential as before. `basalt status` and `basalt upgrade` use the same rule.
- **The per-user config.** `basalt status`, `log`, `approve`, `revert`, `access`, `upgrade`, `passkey list` and `passkey check`, `basalt mcp` (for the status it serves while the engine is down) and `basalt engine`, given no `--config`, use the default path; but when the process is not root, nothing exists at the default path (a missing file only, never a file the person cannot read) and `basalt init` left an `engine.json` in the user's own config directory (`basalt/engine.json` in `os.UserConfigDir`), they read that one instead. A `--config` that is given, even if equal to the default, is used as it is, and root keeps the default. `basalt engine --user NAME` looks in the home of NAME, after it has dropped to NAME, not in the HOME it inherited; like `os.UserConfigDir` (and so `basalt init`) it uses an absolute `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` from its environment, off macOS, so the two agree. Commands that print a follow-up command name the config they used with `--config <absolute path>`. `basalt uninstall` and `basalt passkey remove` read no engine config (they use the admin connection string), so they have no fallback. When the engine runs as a service the unit and the system `engine.json` exist, so the default path is used and the person socket is as above.
- Service mode. **Recording the person.** When `basalt init` installs the engine service it records the person's uid(s) in `engine.json` as `personUids` (an array of integers) and sets `"mode": "SERVICE"`; a config init writes for the foreground engine (`--no-service`, or any of `--config`, `--socket-dir`, `--credential-file`) gets `"mode": "SAME_USER"`. A config without `mode` is `SAME_USER`. The person is `--person-uid N` (repeatable, for a second person), else the user who ran init (`SUDO_UID` when init itself runs under `sudo`, else the user running it). Init passes them to `basalt service install` as `--person-uid N`; `basalt service install` run directly takes `--person-uid`, else `SUDO_UID`. Either refuses to install the service when it has no uid, and refuses uid `0` and the engine's own OS user. Re-running init keeps the recorded uids and adds any new `--person-uid`; nothing removes one but editing `engine.json`. `--person-uid` is a one-shot flag: it is not saved in `initArgs`. When a recorded person uid is also an agent's `osUser` (the default `--os-user` is the person), init warns that while they share that OS user the CLI over the socket sees only that agent's ledger and the agent can use the socket too (the engine cannot tell two processes of one OS user apart; approve and revert still need the passkey signature), and recommends a separate `--os-user` for full isolation (owner decision SM-4).

## Crash reports

Owner decision of 18 Nov 2026 (phase 1 of the crash-report work). When basalt panics it saves a scrubbed report on the machine and tells the person how to read it and file it. **Nothing is sent by basalt**: it has no receiver, makes no network request for a crash and never sends one by itself. The person decides whether to file a GitHub issue.

- **When.** A panic in a `basalt` command (any command, including `engine` and `mcp`), and a panic inside an MCP tool call in the engine. The tool call ends with `INTERNAL` ([Error codes](#error-codes)) instead of the process; its `personMessage` names `basalt crash show <id>` and `basalt crash report <id>`, and its next step is `STOP`. The engine keeps serving after such a call, so a tool must not leave state half-updated when it panics. A panic outside both (another goroutine) and a Go runtime fatal error (an out-of-memory kill, a concurrent map write) cannot be recovered and leave no report yet.
- **Where.** `crash/` under the engine's `stateDir` for a tool call's panic, and under the default state directory for a panic in `basalt engine` itself (mode `0755`, files `0644` before the umask, so the person can read what a service running as another OS user saved; `basalt crash list` and `show` read the default state directory and the per-user `state/crash` next to the person's config), and `crash/` in the person's `basalt` config directory (`~/.config/basalt/crash` on Linux, `~/Library/Application Support/basalt/crash` on macOS; mode `0700`, files `0600`) for every other command. One file per report, `<id>.json`, the id a UUIDv7. **Retention:** each directory keeps the newest **20** reports; saving one more deletes the oldest.
- **The CLI line.** After a command panics, `basalt` prints one line on stderr, `basalt: crashed; a report was saved: see what it holds with `basalt crash show <id>`, file it on GitHub with `basalt crash report <id>``, and exits `1`. When no directory is writable the line says the report could not be saved.
- **The report** (camelCase JSON, `basalt crash show` prints exactly this and nothing else): `id`, `time` (RFC 3339 UTC), `version`, `os`, `arch`, `goVersion`, `command` (a `basalt` command name, or the MCP tool name for an engine crash; `unknown` when it has no such shape), `kind` (`PANIC`; `FATAL` is reserved for the runtime fatal errors above), `errorCode` (`INTERNAL`), `message` and `stack`.
- **Built from an allowlist, then scrubbed.** Free text never enters the report:
  - `command` is a name from the command list, or a tool name matching `^[a-z][a-z0-9_.-]{0,39}$`, never an argument; `errorCode` matches `^[A-Z][A-Z0-9_]{0,63}$`.
  - `message`: for a Go runtime error (`runtime.Error`: an index out of range, a nil dereference) the runtime's own message, cut to 200 bytes; for any other panic value only `panic value of type <T>`, never the value's text (it can hold SQL, rows, a connection string). A message that looks like SQL is replaced by `<redacted>`; quoted text becomes `<text>`.
  - `stack`: only lines of the shapes the Go runtime prints. A function keeps its name and loses its argument list; a source position keeps its last three path components (`cmd/basalt/main.go:73`); `panic:` lines and anything else are dropped. Cut to 16 KiB.
  - Then everything is scrubbed: the home directory (and any `/Users/<name>`, `/home/<name>`, `C:\Users\<name>`) becomes `~`; the values of the environment (six characters or more) become `<env>`; the machine's host name and IP addresses (with or without a port) become `<host>`; URLs and connection strings become `<url>`; `password=`, `host=`, `user=`, `dbname=`, `token=` and similar key=value pairs lose their value; e-mail addresses become `<email>`.
  - So a report holds no SQL text, table or column names, row values, connection strings, passwords, this machine's host name, IP addresses, paths under the home directory or environment values. A test feeds known secrets through every route (panic values of several types, the stack's arguments, paths, the environment) and fails if one survives.
- `basalt crash list`: the saved reports of both directories (the engine's when the person can read it), newest first, one per line: `<id>  <time>  <version>  <command>`; `No crash reports are saved.` when there are none. Read-only; an agent may run it.
- `basalt crash show <id>`: prints the report, exactly what `crash report` puts in the issue. The id is the whole id. Read-only; an agent may run it. An unknown id is exit `1`.
- `basalt crash report <id>`: builds a pre-filled GitHub new-issue link on `https://github.com/BasaltDBHq/BasaltDB/issues/new` (`title` and `body`) and **the person submits the issue**. The title is `crash(<command>): panic INTERNAL in <version>`. The body is the report's fields and its stack in a code block. The link is at most **6000** characters: the stack is cut from the bottom (the top frames say where it crashed), with a note in the body and on stderr. At a terminal (stdin is a terminal) it opens the link in the default browser; with no terminal, or when no browser opens, it prints the link on stdout. **Under an agent** (an agent marker is set, [Agents and the admin credential](#agents-and-the-admin-credential)) it opens nothing and prints no link: it prints that the person should run `basalt crash report <id>` in a terminal of their own.
- **Not here yet** (a later ticket): automatic sending, a receiver, dedupe and a private triage repository. When automatic sending comes it is opt-in (asked once in the person's terminal, saved in config, never from an agent context) with `BASALT_NO_CRASH_REPORTS=1` and a config key to turn it off. Until then there is no switch, because nothing is sent.

## Update notice

People and agents are told when a newer release is out. On by default.

- **The check.** At most once every 24 hours, `basalt` asks `https://api.github.com/repos/BasaltDBHq/BasaltDB/releases` (public, no credentials, 2 seconds, at most 1 MiB). The request carries no data beyond the HTTP request itself (the IP address and a `User-Agent` of `basalt/<version>`): nothing about the person, the machine, the database or an agent. The answer (tag, pre-release flag and security flag of each release, draft releases left out) is cached in `update-check.json` (`0600`) in `$BASALT_CACHE_DIR`, else the `basalt` folder of the user's cache directory. A failed request keeps the old answer and is tried again after an hour. The refresh runs beside the command; a command waits for it at most one second and never fails because of it.
- **Which release counts.** The newest release with a higher version than the running build. A pre-release (a tag with a pre-release suffix such as `-alpha.2`, or a release GitHub flags as pre-release) is offered only when the running build is a pre-release; a tag whose suffix is not made of `[0-9A-Za-z-]` identifiers is not a version and is ignored. A build with no release number (`0.0.0-dev`) gets no notice and makes no request, in the CLI and in the engine. A release is a **security release** when a line of its release notes starts with `Security:` (any case); the notice is a security notice when any newer release that the person may be offered (by the rules above) is one.
- **Off switch.** The environment variable `BASALT_UPDATE_CHECK` set to `off`, `0`, `false` or `no`, or `"updateCheck": "OFF"` in `engine.json` (`ON` or absent leaves it on; the CLI reads the config it would read by default). Off means no request and no notice. In service mode `engine.json` is `0600` and the CLI cannot read it, so the engine reports its setting in the `PAGES` answer (`updateCheck`, `ON` or `OFF`) and the CLI follows it; a config that exists but cannot be read or loaded, with no engine answer within a second, means no request. No config file at all means the check is on.
- **Terminal.** Every `basalt` command except `engine`, `mcp` and help prints one line on stderr after its own output when a notice applies: `basalt <current> is out of date; <latest> is available. <how>` (a security notice reads `is out of date (SECURITY UPDATE)`). `<how>` is `Run: brew upgrade basaltdbhq/tap/basaltdb` when the binary, after symlinks are resolved, sits in a Homebrew Cellar or prefix (`/opt/homebrew`, `/home/linuxbrew/.linuxbrew`), else `Get it at <release page>`. (`basalt upgrade` upgrades the guard in Postgres, not the binary.) The line is yellow, red for a security notice, only when stderr is a terminal and `NO_COLOR` is not set. A command given `--json` prints no line; `basalt status --json` carries `update` instead, in the shape of the `status` tool's (`{ "current", "latest", "security", "url", "message" }`, absent when there is no notice or the check is off): the notice from this binary's own cache, else the engine's `update`; it reads the cache only. The exit code is never changed.
- **Agents.** The engine runs the same check (at start, then when the cache is a day old; cache `update-check.json` in `stateDir`) unless it is off or the build has no release number. When a newer release exists: `status` and `describe` (form `OVERVIEW`) carry `update`: `{ "current", "latest", "security", "url", "message" }`, with `message` starting `WARNING:` (or `SECURITY UPDATE:` for a security notice) and saying what to tell the person and how they upgrade (the brew command, or the release page, then a restart of the engine so it runs the new binary); and the first successful tool result of each MCP connection carries the same `message` as one more text item after the result (once per connection, never on an error result). The `initialize` instructions carry: *"When a result carries a WARNING: or SECURITY UPDATE: notice or an update field, tell the person that a newer BasaltDB release is out and give them the upgrade command it names; never upgrade BasaltDB yourself unless the person says yes."* `basalt mcp` while the engine is down adds nothing.

## The person socket

Service mode only (owner decisions SM-1 to SM-3). With `"mode": "SERVICE"` the engine listens on one more Unix socket, `person.sock` in the socket directory (`/run/basalt/person.sock` on Linux, `/Library/Application Support/Basalt/run/person.sock` on macOS). It serves the person's `basalt log`, `approve`, `revert`, `passkey list`, `passkey check`, `status` and `upgrade`, which cannot read the engine's files in service mode. The engine runs each request with the named agent's own credential and returns rows and prepared text; no response carries the credential, its file name or the config's contents. It serves none of the MCP tools. `person` is reserved: no agent may be named `person`.

- **Access.** The socket file is mode `0666`, like the agent sockets; the socket directory is `0755` and owned by the engine's OS user, so nobody else can replace it. The only access control is the peer uid (`SO_PEERCRED` on Linux, `LOCAL_PEERCRED` on macOS): the engine admits a connection only when the peer's uid is in `personUids`, checked on every connection before a byte is read. Every other uid is refused, an agent's, root's and the engine's own user included; the engine drops `0` and its own uid from the allowlist when it loads it. An empty or missing `personUids` admits nobody. **Agent scope (SM-4).** When the admitted peer's uid is also the `osUser` of one or more agents, every request is scoped to those agents: a request naming any other agent, served or not, is refused with `AGENT_NOT_ALLOWED` before any ledger is read. A peer that is no agent's `osUser` is the person and may name every agent. A connection whose peer credentials cannot be read is closed.
- **Framing.** One request per connection, newline-delimited JSON, UTF-8. After the peer check the engine writes one greeting line: `{"ok":true,"version":1}` when it admits the peer, else `{"ok":false,"version":1,"error":{"code":"UID_NOT_ALLOWED","message":"..."}}` and it closes the connection without reading. The client then writes one request line `{"method","agent","params"}` and reads one response line, `{"result":...}` or `{"error":{"code","message"}}`, and the engine closes the connection. A request over 256 KiB, or one not complete within 10 seconds of the greeting, is refused with `INVALID_ARGUMENT` and the connection closed. Unknown keys in a request or its `params` are refused (`INVALID_ARGUMENT`). The engine serves at most 16 person connections at a time; the next waits in the listen queue.
- **Methods.** `params` and `result` are camelCase JSON as in [Wire format](#wire-format); ids are UUID text, times RFC 3339 UTC, byte strings base64url without padding. Each is the agent-credential call the same-user CLI makes, and nothing more:

| Method | `params` | `result` | Used by |
|---|---|---|---|
| `PAGES` | `{}` | `{ "pagesUrl", "localOrigin", "updateCheck" }`: the pages base URL links use, the origin of the engine's local pages when it serves them (`""` when off), and `"ON"` or `"OFF"`, the engine's update check ([Update notice](#update-notice)) | `approve --show/--open`, `revert`, `passkey check`, the update check of every person command, `status --json` |
| `CHECK_HEALTH` | `{ "full", "pin" }` (`pin` `{ "seq", "hash", "install" }` or null; from [version 36](#version-36-reinstalls-on-the-record) `install` is the guard install it was verified on (the install id, a uuid; the nil uuid for a pin saved before install ids), and `setAside` is the reader's kept copy, `[{ "pinnedSeq", "pinnedHash", "pinnedInstall", "install", "at" }]`, absent when empty) | `{ "chain": { "intact", "brokenAt" }, "head", "uncovered": [{ "table", "why" }], "rlsBlind", "lastHeartbeat", "heartbeatStale", "deadMan", "pii" }`; `pii` (M5.1, absent before guard version 15) is `{ "classified", "unclassified", "unclassifiedNames", "review", "reviewNames", "dictionary" }`, at most 10 names each, and `dictionary` `{ "values", "lastScanAt" }` is the engine's own dictionary status, for the [PII line](#engine-basalt-log-and-basalt-init); `values` counts generic forms, so a two-word `PERSON_NAME`, stored in both orders, counts twice. From [version 36](#version-36-reinstalls-on-the-record) the result also carries `install` (the install checked, to pin with `head`), `pinSetAside` (true when the pin was of another install, did not verify on the current chain, and was recorded with `record_pin_set_aside` on the agent's credential; the new chain is then checked from its start), `pinMoved` (true when it did verify, so the pin moved to the new install and nothing was recorded) and `reinstalls` (the notices' data, newest first, at most 3: `{ "install", "installedAt", "installedBy", "vaultUser", "reports": [{ "agent", "at", "pinnedSeq", "pinnedHash", "more" }], "moreAgents" }`, with `earlierReinstalls` the count past those; `reports` holds the reader's own report first when there is one, otherwise the earliest, then at most 5 other agents' earliest reports, oldest first; `more` counts an agent's further reports and `moreAgents` the agents past those, both absent when 0) | `log` |
| `LIST_CHANGES` | `{ "limit", "cursor", "sort", "runId" }` | `{ "items": [change], "nextCursor" }` | `log` |
| `LIST_ACCESS` | `{}` | `{ "read": [name], "write": [name], "notGranted": [name] }`: schema-qualified table names the agent can read, can write, and cannot read (user schemas only) | `access` |
| `ROW_CHANGES` | `{ "changeIds" }` (at most 200) | `{ "rows": { "<changeId>": [rowChange] } }` | `log --run`, `log --json` |
| `LIST_PASSKEYS` | `{}` | `{ "items": [{ "credentialId", "x", "y", "signCount", "rpId", "origin", "userHandle", "label", "createdAt", "lastUsedAt" }] }`: public keys only | `log`, `approve`, `revert`, `passkey list`, `passkey check` |
| `FIND_APPROVAL` | `{ "approvalId" }` | the held write: `{ "id", "runId", "taskId", "intent", "statement", "params", "assigned", "rows", "status", "createdAt", "expiresAt", "approvedAt", "bindingText", "lines" }` | `approve` |
| `SUBMIT_APPROVAL` | `{ "approvalId", "assertion" }` | `{ "approvedAt" }` | `approve <code>` |
| `FIND_REVERT` | `{ "revertId" }` | `{ "id", "status", "scope", "scopeId", "intent", "rows", "skipped", "expiresAt", "bindingText", "lines", "batched", "signedAt", "applyBy", "batches", "progress": { "restored", "skipped", "pending" }, "changeId" }` | `revert` |
| `PREVIEW_REVERT` | `{ "scope", "id", "runs", "tables" }` | `{ "lines" }` | `revert --dry-run` |
| `PREPARE_REVERT` | `{ "scope", "id", "runs", "tables", "intent" }` | `{ "revertId", "bindingText", "expiresAt", "lines", "batched" }` | `revert` |
| `RESCOPE_REVERT` | `{ "revertId", "runs", "tables" }` | `{ "revertId", "bindingText", "expiresAt", "lines", "batched" }` | `revert --rescope` |
| `REJECT_REVERT` | `{ "revertId", "note" }` | `{ "revertId", "status", "rejectedAt" }` | `revert --reject` |
| `APPLY_REVERT` | `{ "revertId", "assertion" }` | `{ "changeId", "rows", "skipped", "batched", "pending", "applyBy" }` | `revert`, `revert --apply` |
| `LIST_REVERT_LINES` | `{ "revertId", "outcome", "limit", "cursor" }` | `{ "items": [{ "no", "text", "outcome", "conflict", "sqlState", "batch" }], "nextCursor" }` | `revert --status` |
| `STATUS` | `{}` | `{ "status", "engine": { "pid", "osUser" }, "socketDir", "stateDir" }`: `status` is the agent's [`status` tool](#mcp-tools) result | `status`, `upgrade` |
| `TARGET` | `{}` | `{ "host", "port", "database", "supabaseRef" }`: the database the agent's credential connects to, as pgx parses it (`supabaseRef` when its user or host names a Supabase project); no user, password or other parameter | `upgrade` |
| `AGENTS` | `{}` (no `agent` in the request) | `[{ "agent", "host", "database" }]`, sorted by agent: every agent the engine serves this peer with the name and host of its database, nothing else (no user, port, password or credential path). The person's uid gets every agent; an agent's OS user gets only the agents it runs. Not refused by the guard check | `status` without `--agent` |

`STATUS`, `TARGET` and `AGENTS` (added with `basalt status` and `basalt upgrade`) answer while the guard is missing or outdated: unlike every other method they are not refused by the guard check, since they are how the person finds and fixes that.

`assertion` is `{ "credentialId", "authenticatorData", "clientDataJson", "signature" }`, the passkey assertion of the approval or revert code. `scope` is `CHANGE`, `RUN` or `TASK`. `runs` and `tables` (revert scope) are the scope filters of `basalt revert --runs` and `--tables`, `null` or absent for all; the engine builds the descriptor with `agents` = `[the agent's role]`, as for the MCP tools. `RESCOPE_REVERT` keeps the revert's scope and id and calls `rescope_revert` with no proof or token, so it never widens; `REJECT_REVERT` calls `reject_revert` with no token. A `change` and a `rowChange` carry the fields of [the change record](#the-change-record) the CLI prints. `SUBMIT_APPROVAL` and `APPLY_REVERT` are relays: Postgres verifies the passkey signature, as for the same-user CLI, and the socket gives the person no power the agent's credential plus their passkey does not.
- **Errors.** `error.code` is one of `UID_NOT_ALLOWED` (greeting only), `UNKNOWN_AGENT` (the engine serves no agent of that name), `AGENT_NOT_ALLOWED` (the peer is an agent's OS user and named another agent; `message` names the fix, a separate `--os-user`), `UNKNOWN_METHOD`, `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, `NOT_FOUND` (`FIND_APPROVAL`, `FIND_REVERT`: not this agent's), `REFUSED` (Postgres refused the call, and `message` is its message and detail, as the same-user CLI prints them; or the engine refused it before reaching the database because the guard is outdated or missing or the database is down, and `message` is the agent's message for that refusal, with the command that fixes it, such as `basalt upgrade` or `basalt init`) and `INTERNAL` (`message` is generic; the engine logs the cause). The CLI prints `message` the way it prints the same error in same-user mode.

## Setup from your laptop

Planned, M4 (chat-only operator: owner decisions CO-1, CO-9, CO-10, CO-12 to CO-17 and CO-19; owner decisions RI-1 to RI-17). For an engine on a machine the person cannot reach, run by their bot: the bot runs `basalt init --remote` there with no admin credential and gets a setup code; the person runs `basalt init --setup-code <code>` on their own computer with the admin connection string. **The admin connection string never touches a machine the bot controls (CO-9)**: no command on an agent-operated engine's machine asks for it or accepts it.

### Agents and the admin credential

Most people tell their agent "connect to database X" rather than run `basalt init` themselves. Setup must work from that sentence, and the admin connection string must never reach the agent: not in the chat, a file, the environment, a log or an MCP result. The agent-led path is `basalt init --remote` on the agent's side, which needs no admin credential, and `basalt init --setup-code` on the person's own computer, the only place the admin connection string is typed. It is the same for an agent on the person's laptop and a bot on its own machine, and for each further database: a new agent name, its own `--database-url`, the same engine.

- **Agent context.** `basalt` runs under an agent when one of these environment variables is set to a non-blank value: `BASALT_AGENT_CONTEXT` (Basalt's own marker, which a person can set in a bot's environment), `CLAUDECODE`, `CODEX_SANDBOX`, `CODEX_SANDBOX_NETWORK_DISABLED`, `GEMINI_CLI`, `CURSOR_AGENT`, `OPENCODE` (set by coding agents in the shell they run commands in). The first one set, in that order, is the one named in messages.
- **Refused under an agent.** Every command that takes the admin connection string (`basalt init` without `--remote`, `basalt init --setup-code`, `upgrade`, `uninstall`, `classify` and the `passkey` and `engine` subcommands that connect as the admin) refuses under an agent with exit `1` before it reads one: `BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL` is not read and there is no prompt. `basalt init` and `basalt init --setup-code` refuse before parsing their other flags (a usage error included), unless an argument is `-h` or `--help` (then the flags are parsed and help is printed, or a usage error when `-h` is a flag's value; nothing is read either way); the others refuse where they would ask. The message names the marker and says what to do instead, and never echoes a connection string: `<command> needs the database admin connection string, and it is running under an agent (<marker> is set), so basalt does not read one. An agent never asks for, accepts or types the admin connection string or password.`, then, for `basalt init`, to run `basalt init --remote` and give the person the setup code it prints; for every other command, to give the person the command to run in a terminal of their own, which no agent reads.
- **An admin credential in the agent's environment.** When `BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL` is set under an agent, the refusal adds `BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL is set where an agent can read it; basalt did not use it. Remove it from this environment and ask the person to change that database password now.` `basalt init --remote` never uses it either; when it is set, init warns on stderr (`basalt: warning: BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL is set here; basalt init --remote never uses it. If an agent can read this environment, remove it and ask the person to change that database password now.`) and goes on. A `--database-url` with a password is refused as before ([`basalt init --remote`](#basalt-init---remote-the-engines-machine), step 1).
- **A credential in a tool call.** Every MCP tool, before it does anything else (before the engine checks the guard, reaches Postgres or writes the ledger), looks at its arguments: every string, nested ones and object keys included, and the raw request when it is not valid JSON. One that holds a `postgres://` or `postgresql://` URL (the scheme in any case) with a password, in its user part (`postgres://user:secret@host/db`) or as a `password` parameter, is refused with `INVALID_ARGUMENT` reason `ADMIN_CREDENTIAL`. A connection string without a password is not refused. The call changes nothing and is not recorded: no run, no change record, no refusal row, no log line carries the value or any part of it, and neither does the error, whose message is fixed. The next steps tell the agent to ask the person to change that database password now and to call again without the string; the person's message says the same. This is a mistake guard, like the markers above. The engine-down tools of `basalt mcp` (the engine does not answer) refuse the same way. A password in another spelling (a keyword and value string, a percent-encoded `password` parameter name, or split over several arguments) is not detected, and the boundary stays `basalt init --remote`.
- **What an agent reads.** Text written for agents never tells one to run `basalt init` with `--agent` and without `--remote`: the MCP `initialize` instructions ([What the agent is told](#what-the-agent-is-told)), tool descriptions, next steps and the engine's own messages, `basalt --help` (which ends with `Agents: never ask for or accept the database admin connection string or password.` and `To set up, run basalt init --remote and give the person the setup code it prints.`), `basalt init --help` when an agent reads it (an agent marker is set, or stdin is not a terminal; it then shows only the `--remote` and `--setup-code` forms and the rule above), the error for an agent missing from the config (`agent "<name>" is not in <config>; set it up with basalt init --remote --agent <name> and give the person the setup code it prints`), and the agent setup guide. A test reads these surfaces and fails on such a command. A `RUN_COMMAND` step addressed to the `PERSON` on a person-operated engine is the person's command, run in their own terminal, and is not covered by this rule.
- **The honest limit.** The markers stop mistakes, not a determined agent: an agent that shares the person's OS user and terminal can unset them, or read what is typed there, and `sudo` drops them by default (`env_reset`), so a bot with passwordless `sudo` is not detected under it. The boundary is `basalt init --remote` with the admin connection string typed only on the person's own computer, and the engine and the agent running as OS users who cannot read the person's files.

### Who operates the engine

- `basalt init --operated-by=person|agent`, default `person`, is saved in `engine.json` as the top-level `"operatedBy": "PERSON"` or `"AGENT"` (absent means `PERSON`). One engine per machine, one operator. It decides who runs machine steps ([Next steps](#next-steps)) and is independent of how setup runs: the chat-only case is `--operated-by=agent --remote`; a person may also run init with the admin credential on a machine they control and hand that engine to a bot. `basalt init --agent NAME --operated-by=agent` (no `--remote`) does exactly that: it writes `"operatedBy": "AGENT"`, uses the portal's pages (`--passkey-origin` defaults to `https://portal.basaltvault.app`, and any other origin is a usage error unless `--dev-local-pages`, [Page origins](#page-origins)), turns the Vault on unless `--vault=off`, saves `--operated-by=agent` in `initArgs`, and, when it installs the service, passes `--operated-by` to `service install --agent`. An explicit `--operated-by=person` writes `"operatedBy": "PERSON"`, so an engine can be switched back; without the flag the config's value stays. `basalt init --help` names `--operated-by` and the other two modes, `--remote` and `--setup-code`, for a person at a terminal; when an agent reads it, it shows only the agent-led setup ([Agents and the admin credential](#agents-and-the-admin-credential)).
- With `AGENT`, `pagesUrl` is the portal `https://portal.basaltvault.app` (CO-6, CO-8): the loopback pages would belong to the bot's machine, so a local `pagesUrl` or `--passkey-origin` is refused, except for development with `--dev-local-pages` ([Page origins](#page-origins)).
- **In the ledger (CO-15).** Every `HEARTBEAT` row's detail carries `operatedBy` next to `engineVersion` (and `chainBrokenAt` when the chain is broken), so the ledger shows the periods when enforcement was guard-only. A change shows at the next heartbeat.

### `basalt init --remote` (the engine's machine)

`basalt init --remote --agent NAME [--write schema.table,…] [--read schema.table,…] [--prefix P] [--statement-timeout D] [--operated-by person|agent] [--database-url URL] [--new-code] [--config PATH] [--json]`

0. **A release version.** The code carries the build's version, which must be `major.minor.patch` with an optional pre-release (a leading `v` is dropped). A build that reports anything else (a source build without tags reports `dev`) is refused before anything is written, with exit `1` and `this build of basalt has no release version (it reports "dev"), so it cannot make a setup code; install a release build`.
1. **No admin credential.** `--database-url` is the connection string without a password (Supabase shows it with a `[YOUR-PASSWORD]` placeholder), required on the agent's first run and read from the credential file after that. A URL with a password, in the user part or as a `password` parameter, is refused before anything else (exit `1`), and never written, logged or echoed: `This connection string contains a password. basalt init --remote does not need it and it must not be shared with an agent: change your database password now.` The host, port and database come from the URL itself (`postgres://user@host:port/database`): a `host`, `hostaddr`, `port` or `dbname` parameter is refused (exit `2`), so the setup code and the agent's connection name the same database. Login parameters of the URL (`user`, `passfile`, `service`, `sslcert`, `sslkey` and the like) never reach the agent's credential file. `BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL` is ignored and there is no prompt.
2. **The agent's password stays here.** Init generates it (256 bits, as `basalt init` does), writes the agent's connection string (user `<prefix>_agent_<name>`, or `<prefix>_agent_<name>.<ref>` through Supabase's pooler) to the `0600` credential file, and puts only its SCRAM-SHA-256 verifier in the code. Because the verifier is sensitive ([The setup code](#the-setup-code)), the connection string it writes keeps the strongest protection init can confirm, on by default, in this order: `sslmode=verify-full` with channel binding (`channel_binding=require`, SCRAM-SHA-256-PLUS, bound to the server's TLS certificate); `sslmode=verify-full` alone, for a server whose certificate verifies but that offers no channel binding (PgBouncer, Supabase's pooler); `sslmode=verify-ca` (when the connection string had it) or `sslmode=require`, with channel binding; else the connection string as built. A `verify-ca` or `verify-full` the connection string already had is never weakened. Only a verified certificate stops an active attacker who holds the verifier: with `sslmode=require` they can pose as the server with their own certificate and recover the `ClientKey` from the agent's proof, so channel binding there only stops a plaintext downgrade and a relayed session. Init tries each first with a login bound to fail (the new password is not set yet). With channel binding only a refused password (`28P01`) counts, which proves TLS and channel binding work; `verify-full` alone counts on any refusal of the login (SQLSTATE class `28`, or Supabase's pooler answering `Tenant or user not found` for a role it does not know yet), since the client sends its login only after a TLS handshake whose certificate verified for the host name. On Supabase's pooler (`*.pooler.supabase.com`) that is the pooler's own certificate, the one the agent's connection checks. When none counts (no TLS, a Unix socket, `sslmode=disable`, a certificate that does not verify and no channel binding, or the database is not reachable), it keeps the connection string as built, since a credential that cannot log in is worse; a probe that reaches no address of the server (every address failed to connect or timed out) ends the probing. Init prints one line saying what it applied, for example `basalt: connection protected: database identity verified (channel binding not available through the Supabase pooler)`, or `basalt: connection not protected further: ...` with the reason, the `sslmode` kept and, when no certificate verified, a hint to add `sslrootcert`; a certificate signed by a CA the system does not trust verifies when the connection string names it with `sslrootcert`. Each run leaves up to three failed logins in the server's log (some hosts ban an address after many); running init again probes again, as when the server's certificate changes. A new password for an agent whose role already logs in goes to `<credentialFile>.pending` (`0600`) instead: the old one keeps working until the person runs the code, and the engine swaps the files when it sees the setup recorded (below).
3. **The kind of code.** `INSTALL` when the agent's role cannot log in with its credential (or there is none yet). Otherwise `AGENT` when `--write`, `--read` or `--statement-timeout` change, or with `--new-code` (then with a new password and verifier); else `UPGRADE` when the installed guard (read with the agent credential, which every guard version allows) is older than this build's; else an `AGENT` code with no change and no verifier (it re-applies the grants, and the person's computer registers a portal passkey when none is active). A code of any kind also applies the migrations the database lacks up to `guard.to`.
4. **One pending code per agent.** While an unexpired code is pending and nothing changed, init prints the same code again (`reused: true`: same `setupId`, same expiry). A change or `--new-code` makes a new one with a new `setupId` and expiry; the old one is then refused by the person's computer once the new one has run (newer setup, below). An expired code is replaced by a new one.
5. **Config.** Init writes `engine.json` as `basalt init` does (`initArgs`, [CLI](#cli)), plus `operatedBy`, `pagesUrl` and, per agent, the pending setup: `"setup": { "setupId", "kind", "guardVersion", "expiresAt", "code", "fingerprint" }` (`guardVersion` is the code's `guard.to`; `code` is the setup code, kept so `status` and a re-run hand out the same one). Nothing secret goes in `engine.json`. `setupId` is a UUIDv7 of the clock now, and `expiresAt` is its time plus 24 hours, truncated to the second.
6. **Output.** Exit `0`: the setup is pending, not failed. Text: the code in a block of its own, its [fingerprint](#the-setup-code), and what the person does with it. `--json`: [`remote-init-result.schema.json`](api/mcp/setup/remote-init-result.schema.json), with `fingerprint`, `personMessage` (install `basalt` from the official release page on their own computer, run the command, check that it shows the same fingerprint, never paste the database password into the chat) and `nextSteps`: P `RUN_COMMAND` (`basalt init --setup-code <code>`, on their own computer), P `TELL_AGENT`, A `END_TURN`, A `CALL_TOOL` (`status`). A code longer than 1500 characters is refused (exit `1`), saying to name fewer tables.
7. Init does not install or start the engine: on an agent-operated machine the agent does it (an `AGENT` `RUN_COMMAND`).
8. **As root** (`sudo basalt init --remote`). The config's directory may belong to the engine's OS user, so root trusts nothing that user could redirect, and refuses (exit `1`, nothing written) before reading or writing any credential: a config that is not a regular file with one link, is reached through a symlink anyone but root controls (root-owned links on the way, such as macOS's `/etc`, are followed), is owned by anyone but root or its directory's owner, or is writable by group or others (`... is writable by group or others (mode 0666), so another user could change what root trusts in it; remove that access (sudo chmod go-w ...)`); a config whose agent names a `store.credentialFile` other than `agents/<agent>.url` next to the config, the only path `basalt init` and `basalt service install` write (`the config names the credential file X, not Y where basalt init puts it; as root, basalt init --remote uses only that path. Fix the agent's store.credentialFile in the config`). A `store.credentialFile` that differs only in spelling (`agents/sub/../bot.url`) is accepted, and root then uses the path it computed, never the config's string, and writes that path back to the config as an absolute path (also with a relative `--config`). The `agents/` directory is opened once from `/` without following a symlink anyone but root controls, every directory on the way owned by root or the config directory's owner (when the config directory does not exist yet, the owner of the nearest directory above it that does) and not writable by group or others (made `0700` when missing), and the credential file, its `.pending` and its `.lock` are then read, written and renamed relative to that directory: an `agents/` swapped for a symlink is refused (`... is not a real directory (a symlink is refused)`), on the first run as on later ones. The config directory is opened the same way, once, and `engine.json` is locked (its `.lock`), read (the checks above again) and written relative to it, so a config directory swapped for a symlink between the hardened read and the write (by a user who owns a directory above it) redirects nothing; a symlinked `.lock` there or in `agents/` is refused as a lock file that cannot be used. The `engine.json` root writes there is handed to the config directory's owner when that owner is not root (set on the new file's descriptor before it is renamed into place), as the files in `agents/` are, and a directory root makes on the way (`agents/`, or the config directory itself) is handed to the trusted owner when its parent belongs to that owner. So on a service engine's machine (`basalt service install` made the config directory, owned by the engine's OS user) `engine.json`, a new `agents/` and every file in it are the engine user's, and the service reads them. Root also records the user who ran sudo (`SUDO_UID`, never `0`) as a person the engine's [person socket](#the-person-socket) admits, added to the config's `personUids`, so `basalt status --agent`, `log`, `approve` and `revert` work there for that user; without `SUDO_UID` it records none. With `SUDO_UID` set, a config directory owned by a user other than root and the one who ran sudo is an engine user's, so root writes the config with `"mode": "SERVICE"` (the engine then serves the person socket) and, once `engine.json` is written, says so on stderr (`basalt: <dir> belongs to uid N, not root or you (uid P): this config is a service one (mode SERVICE). ...`); a config directory the user who ran sudo owns stays theirs, in same-user mode. Every file root makes there (the credential file, its `.pending` and its `.lock`) is handed to the owner of `agents/`, read from the opened directory, when that owner is not root (the engine's user in service mode, as for the [lock files](#on-the-engine)): the owner is set on the new file's descriptor before it is renamed into place, so the engine reads its credential and promotes a `.pending` one. Root reads only credential files owned by root or that owner, and from one that owner wrote it takes only a string starting exactly `postgres://` or `postgresql://`, with a non-empty password, no `#`, `;`, space or control character, and no parameter but `application_name`, `channel_binding`, `connect_timeout`, `default_query_exec_mode`, `max_protocol_version`, `min_protocol_version`, `require_auth`, `sslmode`, `sslnegotiation`, `sslsni` and `target_session_attrs`, checked with the Postgres driver's own parser as well, whose host, port, database and password must match the URL's (exit `1` otherwise, never echoing the string). Every other parameter is refused, among them those that make the driver read a file for root (`passfile`, `service`, `servicefile`, `sslkey`, `sslcert`, `sslpassword`, `sslrootcert`, `sslcrl`), as is a URL without a password, which falls back to root's `~/.pgpass`: that owner could otherwise have root read a root-only file, or send a password from root's `~/.pgpass` to a server of their choosing. Such a credential is still usable by `basalt init --remote` run as that user.

### The setup code

`basalt-setup.1.<payload>.<check>`, one line, shown in a code block (so chat apps do not read `_` as emphasis).

- `payload`: base64url without padding of deflate-raw of the JSON in [`setup-code.schema.json`](api/mcp/setup/setup-code.schema.json): `format` (1), `setupId` (UUIDv7), `kind`, `prefix`, `target`, `guard` (`from`, `to`), `build`, `agent` (`name`, `write`, `read`, `verifier`, `statementTimeoutMs`; absent for `UPGRADE`), `expiresAt`, and from M4.1 `engine` (`publicKey`; optional, below). Never the agent's password, never anything of the admin; the one sensitive field is `agent.verifier` (below). Encoders write compact JSON in the schema's key order, deflated at level 9; decoders accept any raw deflate stream and any key order.
- `check`: base64url without padding of the first 4 bytes of sha256 over the bytes before the last `.` (`basalt-setup.1.<payload>`), 6 characters.
- **Decoding**, in order: drop every whitespace character (a wrapped copy still works); not `basalt-setup.` → `NOT_A_SETUP_CODE` (`This is not a Basalt setup code.`); `basalt-setup.1` with nothing after it → `INVALID`; a version other than `1.` → `UNSUPPORTED_FORMAT`; a check that does not match → `DAMAGED` (`This setup code is damaged; copy it again.`); a payload that is not base64url, inflates past 64 KiB, is not JSON, holds a key twice in one object (at any depth; a decoder that kept the first or the last would read different codes) or fails the schema → `INVALID`; `format` other than 1 → `UNSUPPORTED_FORMAT` (`This setup code is newer than basalt on this computer: update it from the release page.`). The golden fixtures [`setup-code.fixtures.json`](api/mcp/setup/setup-code.fixtures.json) pin each case, and pin the verifier for a password and salt; the contract test checks them.
- **Rules the schema cannot state:** `guard.from` ≤ `guard.to` (`<` for `UPGRADE`); at least one table in `write` or `read`; no table in both; the role `<prefix>_agent_<name>` at most 63 bytes; `expiresAt` no later than the `setupId`'s UUIDv7 time (its first 48 bits, milliseconds since the epoch) plus 24 hours. Failing one is `INVALID`. These do not depend on the clock; the clock checks are the person's computer's and Postgres's (below).
- **Fingerprint.** `sha256(utf8("basalt-setup-fingerprint.2\n" + payload))`, where `payload` is the decoded payload written back as the compact JSON an encoder writes (the schema's key order, absent keys left out, no HTML escaping), so it covers every field of the code (agent name, tables, verifier, kind, statement timeout, target, guard, build, expiry) whatever key order or deflate stream the code-maker used; its first 10 bytes (80 bits, too many to grind a matching substitute within the 24 hours) in lowercase hex as five groups of four (`3f9a 21c0 7d44 e1b2 90ac`). `basalt init --remote` prints it, `status` returns it while the setup is pending, the agent's `personMessage` names it, and `basalt init --setup-code` shows it before asking. It lets the person check that the code they run is the one their own agent made, not one another chat member posted, or reposted with anything changed. It is a matching aid: it proves nothing about who made the code.
- **`verifier`**: `SCRAM-SHA-256$4096:<salt>$<StoredKey>:<ServerKey>` (RFC 5802 and RFC 7677, standard base64): salt 16 random bytes, 4096 iterations, `SaltedPassword` = PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA-256 of the password, `StoredKey` = sha256(HMAC(`SaltedPassword`, `Client Key`)), `ServerKey` = HMAC(`SaltedPassword`, `Server Key`). Postgres stores a verifier given as `PASSWORD` as is, whatever `password_encryption` says. **The verifier is sensitive.** It is not the password, but whoever holds it and sees one SCRAM exchange of the role (a connection without TLS, or TLS the client does not verify) can derive the `ClientKey` and log in as the agent; with the `ServerKey` they can also pose as the server to the agent. So the code goes only from the agent to the person, and the agent's connection string is hardened ([`basalt init --remote`](#basalt-init---remote-the-engines-machine), step 2).
- **Kinds (CO-14).** `INSTALL`: the guard, a **new** agent role with its password from the verifier, its grants, a portal passkey; a role that already exists is refused (the code-maker would otherwise set the password of a role that is not theirs). `AGENT`: an existing agent's tables (a role that does not exist is refused), replaced by `write` and `read` as given (grants on tables no longer listed are revoked); with `verifier` its password is rotated, without it kept. `UPGRADE`: migrations only; password, grants and policies unchanged.
- **`target`**: the database the bot's engine connects to (`host`, `port`, `database`, and `supabaseRef` when the connection string names a Supabase project: a pooler user `postgres.<ref>` or a host `db.<ref>.supabase.co`).
- **Valid for 24 hours, single use (CO-13).** `expiresAt` is 24 hours after `basalt init --remote` made the code. Because the code-maker writes `expiresAt` and `setupId`, neither is trusted as given: the person's computer (its clock) and `record_setup` (the database's `clock_timestamp()`, from version 18; `now()` before) refuse an expired code, a `setupId` whose time is more than the **clock skew of 5 minutes** ahead of now, and an `expiresAt` more than 24 hours plus 5 minutes ahead of now. Expiry itself is checked without skew: a code is expired from `expiresAt` on. Single use is enforced in Postgres (`basalt_meta.setups`, version 16): the person's computer records `setupId` in the install's transaction. A code is also refused when the agent's latest recorded setup is newer (UUIDv7 ids are time-ordered), so an older unused code cannot reset the password to an old verifier.
- **`engine`** (M4.1, CO-18): `{ "publicKey" }`, the engine's signing key as its SPKI in base64url (122 characters, [Engine keys](#engine-keys)), optional in every kind. `basalt init --remote` adds it whenever the engine's key file loads; when the file is missing it creates it first, as the engine does on its first start (owned by the engine's OS user, mode `0600`), and when the file is refused (`KEY_ERROR`), or this OS user cannot read or create it (init run as another user than the engine's), it leaves `engine` out and says why on stderr, naming the user to run it as. As root (`sudo basalt init --remote`, a service engine's machine) it reads or creates the key file relative to the state directory opened from `/` without following a symlink anyone but root controls. The state directory comes from `engine.json`, which the config directory's owner may write, so it is trusted no further than the config directory: every directory on the way must be owned by root or the config directory's owner and not writable by group or others, the state directory itself must be owned by the config directory's owner, and init never makes it (the service manager makes the default one when the service starts; when it is missing init says to start the service once, or, for a state directory elsewhere (a config given with `--config` keeps its state next to it), to make it for the config directory's owner with `sudo install -d -m 0700 -o <uid> <dir>`). The file must be a regular file with one link owned by that owner, mode `0600` or narrower, and pass the engine's own checks; a new one is written whole for that owner (given to it before it appears under its name), and one the engine made meanwhile is kept. It is in the payload, so the setup fingerprint covers it. A rule the schema cannot state: a `publicKey` that is not a P-256 SPKI with an uncompressed point on the curve is `INVALID`. The format stays 1: a laptop older than this refuses a code with `engine` as `INVALID` (its schema is closed), and the person updates `basalt`.

### `basalt init --setup-code` (the person's own computer)

`basalt init --setup-code <code> [--yes] [--agent-rls=ask|all-rows|none] [--add-passkey] [--passkey-label LABEL] [--engine-url ADDRESS] [--dry-run]`. macOS and Linux only in M4 (CO-19). It writes no engine config and no credential file and never touches a local engine's config.

1. **Decode and validate** as above. Then, by this computer's clock, exit `1` with nothing changed, in this order: `EXPIRED`, now at or past `expiresAt` (`This setup code expired on <time UTC>. Ask <agent> for a new one.`); `SETUP_ID_IN_FUTURE`, the `setupId`'s time more than 5 minutes ahead of now, and `EXPIRY_TOO_FAR`, `expiresAt` more than 24 hours and 5 minutes ahead of now (both `This setup code claims a time in the future. Check this computer's clock, or ask <agent> for a new code.`); `BINARY_TOO_OLD`, `guard.to` newer than this binary's guard (`Update basalt on this computer to at least <build>.`); `GUARD_TOO_OLD`, `guard.to` older than 1, the first version with `record_setup`, which a valid code never is (`This setup code is for guard version <to>, but setup codes need guard version 1 or later. Ask <agent> for a new code made with an up-to-date basalt.`).
2. **The admin connection string** from `BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL` or a hidden prompt, never written anywhere.
3. **Target (CO-17).** The admin connection string must name the same database as `target`: with `supabaseRef`, the same project ref (from the pooler user or the direct host) and database name, so the pooler and the direct host of one project match; otherwise the same host, port and database. A mismatch is refused, never asked about (exit `1`, nothing changed): `This setup code is for database <database> of <Supabase project <ref> | <host>:<port>> (the agent connects there). Your admin connection string points at <...>. Either run this command with the admin connection string of <...>, or ask <agent> for a new code for the database you meant (basalt init --remote --new-code --database-url <that database, without password>).`
4. **The database's state**, read with the admin connection string, refused in this order (exit `1`, nothing changed): `PREFIX_MISMATCH`, `basalt_meta` is installed with another prefix (its owner role is not `<prefix>_guard`: `This setup code is for prefix <prefix>, but this database's Basalt uses <installed>.`); `USED` and `SUPERSEDED` (read from `basalt_meta.setups`, and enforced again by `record_setup`: `This setup code was already used on <time UTC>. Ask <agent> for a new one.`, or `A newer setup for <agent> already ran on <time UTC>. Ask <agent> for a new code.`); `ROLE_EXISTS`, an `INSTALL` code whose role already exists (`<role> already exists. This code would replace its password: ask <agent> for a new code, or run basalt init --remote --new-code for that agent if it is yours.`), also enforced in Postgres, since the `INSTALL` SQL creates the role with a plain `CREATE ROLE`, never `IF NOT EXISTS`; `ROLE_MISSING`, an `AGENT` code whose role does not exist. The fixtures' `laptop` cases pin this order. Then an `UPGRADE` code whose `guard.to` is at or below the installed guard's version has nothing to do: exit `0`, nothing changed and nothing recorded (`basalt: the guard is already at version <installed> and this code upgrades it to version <to>: nothing to upgrade; nothing changed`).
5. **Show, then ask.** First the summary, whose lines are fixed so the person reads the same words every time: by kind, `This creates the agent <name> (role <role>). Its password comes from the code: only whoever made this code can log in as <role>.` (`INSTALL`), `This replaces the password of the existing agent <name> (role <role>): only whoever made this code can then log in as <role>. Run it only if <name> itself gave you this code.` (`AGENT` with a verifier), `This changes the tables of the existing agent <name> (role <role>); its password stays.` (`AGENT` without), or `This upgrades Basalt's guard from version <from> to <to>; no agent changes.`; then `Prefix <prefix>, statement timeout <n> ms, at most 8 connections.` (for `AGENT`, with `(now <m> ms)` after the timeout when the role's current `statement_timeout` differs, and `(<m> ms set for this database only, which takes precedence)` when the role has a different `statement_timeout` for the connected database (`ALTER ROLE ... IN DATABASE`), both joined by `; ` when both apply; `UPGRADE`: `Prefix <prefix>.`); then `Setup fingerprint: <fingerprint>. Check that <name> shows the same fingerprint.` (`your agent` for `UPGRADE`). Then in words what the agent gets (`<agent> will be able to read … and change rows in …, up to 10 rows at a time without asking you, 11 to 50 with your passkey, never more. It cannot change your tables' structure.`), tables outside `public` flagged (for Supabase, `auth` and `storage` more strongly); then the row-level security question per table, as `basalt init` asks it (`--agent-rls`); then the SQL (the role's password shown as the verifier); then `Run this? [y/N]` unless `--yes`. **All or nothing (CO-16):** every requested table or none; to change the list the bot makes a new code. `--dry-run` prints and runs nothing.
6. **One transaction:** the missing migrations, the role (`CREATE ROLE ... PASSWORD '<verifier>'` for `INSTALL`, `ALTER ROLE ... PASSWORD '<verifier>'` for `AGENT` with a verifier), the grants, the confirmed agent policies, `attach`, and `basalt_meta.record_setup`. Any failure leaves the database as it was.
7. **Passkey.** When no passkey registered on the portal is active, or with `--add-passkey`: init prints and opens `https://portal.basaltvault.app/passkeys/register#1.<request>`, reads the pasted `basalt-passkey.1.` code, verifies it and stores it with the admin credential, as `basalt init` does.
8. Ends with `Done. Tell <agent> to check its status.`, exit `0`.

**The engine key and address (M4.1; CO-18, VC-27, [Direct transport](#direct-transport)).** When the code carries `engine`, laptop init also records the engine, in the steps above:

- **Step 1.** `--engine-url` is checked by the [address rules](#direct-transport) before anything else (a usage error, exit `2`, naming the rule it breaks). `--engine-url` with a code that has no `engine` is `ENGINE_KEY_MISSING` (exit `1`, nothing changed): `This setup code carries no engine key, so basalt cannot record the engine address. Ask <agent> to check basalt status (its key file may be refused) and make a new code.`
- **Step 4**, last, when `basalt_meta.engines` exists, and before the "nothing to upgrade" exit: the code's engine id revoked is `ENGINE_REVOKED` (exit `1`, nothing changed): `Engine <fingerprint> was revoked in this database on <time UTC>. A revoked engine is never recorded again: ask <agent> to run basalt engine rotate-key and make a new code.` An `UPGRADE` code with nothing to upgrade still goes on when its engine is not recorded, or is recorded with another address than `--engine-url`: it then records only the engine and the setup.
- **Step 5**, after the setup fingerprint: `This records engine <fingerprint> for <agent>.` (`your agent` for `UPGRADE`; an `UPGRADE` with nothing to upgrade replaces its upgrade line with `No guard change.`) plus ` Its address: <address>.` with `--engine-url` (` (now <old address>)` when the engine is recorded with another one), or ` Its address stays <address>.` when one is recorded and none given, then `Check that <agent>'s basalt status shows the same engine fingerprint; your devices will pin this one.` When the database has revoked any engine, it then says `This database revoked engine <fingerprint> on <time UTC>. Recording a new engine trusts the agent's server again: do it only if you rebuilt or replaced that server.` (the latest revocation). Then, for each other engine not revoked whose recorded address is the one given: `Engine <fingerprint> is also recorded at <address> (since <time UTC>). Revoke it? [y/N]`; with `--yes` none is revoked, and init says so on stderr.
- **Step 6.** `basalt_meta.record_engine(<key>, <address or null>)` in the same transaction, after the migrations, and `revoke_engine` for each engine the person chose to revoke.
- **Step 8** also prints, when an address was recorded: `Pin it on each device you approve from: https://portal.basaltvault.app/engines/add` on its own line.

A code without `engine` and without `--engine-url` records no engine, as before.

### On the engine

- **Serving while an `INSTALL` setup is pending.** An agent whose `engine.json` has a pending `INSTALL` `setup` is served degraded: `status` works, and every other tool answers `UNAVAILABLE` reason `SETUP_PENDING` (`SETUP_EXPIRED` after `expiresAt`) with the [steps](#next-steps-per-code), the pending `code` filled in. A pending `AGENT` or `UPGRADE` setup does not change serving; `status` reports `SETUP_CHANGE_PENDING`. An expired `AGENT` or `UPGRADE` setup is dropped from `engine.json`, and the problem that called for it comes back with its steps.
- **Finishing.** On `status`, on every call while degraded, and at each heartbeat, the engine logs in with the agent's credential (the `.pending` one first, when there is one) and looks for the pending `setupId` in `basalt_meta.agent_setups`. Found: it moves a `.pending` credential over the credential file, clears `setup` from `engine.json`, and serves normally; `setup.state` is `DONE`. Logging in proves the role exists with the bot's password: only the bot knows it, and the verifier came from the bot. Guard version, grants and passkeys are then `status`'s usual checks (`GUARD_OUTDATED`, `PERMISSION_DENIED` on use, `NO_PASSKEY`). Details: the engine moves the `.pending` credential and writes `engine.json` (atomically, only when the agent's pending `setupId` is still the one found, never through a symlink, under `<config>.lock`, the config-wide lock every read-modify-write of `engine.json` by `basalt init` takes too) while it holds `<credentialFile>.lock`, the lock `basalt init --remote` takes; it moves `.pending` only when that path still names the file that logged in (a regular file with one link, owned by the engine's user, mode `0600`), and only when it names the same host, port, database and user as the engine's credential (a code for another database is moved over by the next `basalt init --remote`, and the engine uses it once restarted); the engine's connections then log in with the new password, with no restart. A held lock or a failed move leaves the setup pending in memory and the next call or heartbeat tries again; a config the engine cannot write is logged, and the agent is served all the same, since the database, not the file, says the setup ran.
- **Lock files.** `<config>.lock` and `<credentialFile>.lock` belong to the engine's OS user, mode `0600`, so the engine can always take them. Writers that run as root take them too and never leave one the engine cannot open: `basalt service install --agent` holds `<config>.lock` from reading `engine.json` until the new file is in place, and a lock file that a root process creates, or finds owned by root, is handed to the owner of its directory when that owner is not root (in service mode the engine's user owns the config and credential directories). A lock file the engine cannot open (permissions, owner, not a regular file) is not retried in silence: the engine logs it and `status` reports the problem `LOCK_FILE_UNUSABLE` (`WARNING`, its `personMessage` naming the file) for as long as the next try fails; a `.pending` credential is not promoted meanwhile. Its fix is a root command on the engine's machine, so it is a `PERSON` step on an agent-operated engine too.
- **The shim** reads `operatedBy` and `setup` from the engine config when the engine is down; when it cannot read the file it assumes `PERSON` and no pending setup.
- **Fingerprint in messages.** While a setup is pending, `status` returns `setup.fingerprint`, and every `personMessage` that hands the person the setup code names the fingerprint and asks them to check that `basalt init --setup-code` shows the same.

### Controls

What stops a setup code being used against the person (design section 10):

- **Another agent's role.** `INSTALL` never touches an existing role (`ROLE_EXISTS`, and `CREATE ROLE` in Postgres); a code that sets an existing role's password says so in the summary, naming the agent and role.
- **Old or forged-time codes.** Expiry, a `setupId` in the future and an `expiresAt` too far ahead are refused by the person's clock and by the database's (5 minutes of skew); single use and order are enforced by `record_setup`.
- **A substitute code** posted in the chat by someone else, with their own verifier: the fingerprint shown on the person's computer must match the one their agent reports (`status`, `personMessage`).
- **Wrong database**: target mismatch is refused (CO-17). **Wrong prefix**: refused (`PREFIX_MISMATCH`).
- **The admin credential** is used only on the person's computer (CO-9); the SQL comes from the person's own `basalt` binary, never from the code.
- **An agent running `basalt`** with the admin credential: refused under an agent marker ([Agents and the admin credential](#agents-and-the-admin-credential)), within the limit stated there.

## Approvals

M2. This section fixes the bytes. JSON Schemas for every payload are in [`api/mcp/approval/`](api/mcp/approval/).

- An approval or revert counts only with a valid P-256 passkey signature (WebAuthn ES256), verified inside Postgres. Pages and relays (the engine, the CLI, the agent's chat) carry signed data and are not trusted.
- **Pages.** Static files under `web/`. Hosted (not live yet) on the portal `https://portal.basaltvault.app` with passkey RP ID `basaltvault.app` (fixed for good: registered passkeys depend on it) and origin `https://portal.basaltvault.app`. The origins a page may run on are a closed list ([Page origins](#page-origins)). Until hosting exists the default everywhere is the engine's local pages, `http://localhost:8787` with RP ID `localhost` (owner decisions, Decided Nov 12). Both have the same paths ([Page links](#page-links)). The clientDataJSON origin check stays strict: a passkey signs only on the origin it was registered on. The pages have no backend, load no third-party code, and send nothing over the network: input comes in the URL fragment, output is a code the person copies. From M4.1 the engine also serves them on loopback with a same-origin relay ([Local pages](#local-pages)); that listener follows the listener rules in [AGENTS.md](AGENTS.md) and the `websec` hardening; custom HTTP headers use the lowercase **`basalt-`** prefix.
- **Expiry.** 24 hours after the hold. No answer means blocked.
- **Engine.** On `BS002` the engine checks the write is repeatable (`NOT_REPEATABLE` otherwise), previews it, holds it, and answers `APPROVAL_REQUIRED` with the link. The engine config (`engine.json`) may set `pagesUrl` (the pages base URL, default `http://localhost:8787`; the older key `approvalPage` is still read, its origin only) and `pagesAddr` (see [Local pages](#local-pages)).
