Version 2 gives each install a random id; the objects are described in [Version 36](#version-36-reinstalls-on-the-record).

| Object | Change |
|---|---|
| `installs` | Replaced: one row (`id boolean` primary key, always true) with `install uuid`. The upgrade keeps the time, user and Vault user of the row for the current schema oid and gives it a random id; with no such row (`basalt_meta` restored outside the install) it writes a new one, as `basalt init` does |
| `install_id()` | New, granted to `<prefix>_agents` |
| `record_pin_set_aside(bigint, bytea, oid)` | Dropped. Replaced by `record_pin_set_aside(bigint, bytea, uuid)`, which also refuses with detail `NO_INSTALL` |
| `agent_reinstalls` | Recreated with `pinned_install` and `install` as `uuid` (the grant is kept). Ledger rows are never rewritten: a report recorded at version 1 keeps its oids in `detail`, and the view shows each such oid as the uuid `00000000-0000-0000-0000-` followed by the oid in 12 hex digits, which no random id is, so the earlier reports of one install still group together |

`basalt init` and `basalt upgrade` write the install row with the statement of the version the guard is at after the migrations, so an install that stops at version 1 still writes version 1's row. After the upgrade, a pin saved at version 1 carries no install id and is settled as [Version 36](#version-36-reinstalls-on-the-record) describes: on an unchanged ledger it moves to the new id with the warning, once.

### Guard errors

Raised by the guard with SQLSTATE class `BS`. The tool code each maps to is in [Error codes](#error-codes), the only mapping table.

| SQLSTATE | Message | When |
|---|---|---|
| `BS001` | `INTENT_REQUIRED` | Agent write without `basalt.intent` |
| `BS002` | `APPROVAL_REQUIRED` | 11 to 50 rows, or any `DELETE`. without a signed approval (`basalt.approval_id`); the engine then previews and holds it |
| `BS003` | `ROW_LIMIT_EXCEEDED` | Over 50 rows in one action, cascades included |
| `BS004` | `STALE_READ` | An agent `UPDATE` or `DELETE` of a row whose `xmin` differs from the version in `reads` for the run in `basalt.run_id`. Detail names the table, key and both versions. Checked before the row policy |
| `BS005` | `APPROVAL_INVALID`, `APPROVAL_EXPIRED`, `REVERT_INVALID`, `REVERT_EXPIRED` | Bad approval, revert authorisation or signature |
| `BS006` | `PREVIEW_CANNOT_COMMIT` | A preview transaction tried to commit |
| `BS007` | `NOT_ALLOWED` | `TRUNCATE`, unknown `basalt.mode`, a run of another agent; from version 37 a held write's preview lines over 64 MiB (detail `HOLD_TOO_LARGE`, the hint gives the size) |
| `BS008` | `LEDGER_INPUT_INVALID` | A ledger function got invalid input |
| `BS009` | `NOTHING_TO_REVERT`, `REVERT_CASCADE` | M3. A revert that cannot be prepared; `REVERT_CASCADE` is caught inside the revert and becomes a `CASCADE` conflict. `REVERT_TOO_LARGE` is removed in version 9 |
| `BS010` | `SETUP_REFUSED` | Planned, version 16. `record_setup` refused a setup code; detail `USED`, `SUPERSEDED`, `EXPIRED`, `SETUP_ID_IN_FUTURE` or `EXPIRY_TOO_FAR`. Raised to the admin's `basalt init --setup-code` only, never to an agent |
| `BS011` | `SCOPE_REFUSED` | Version 20. A revert scope descriptor refused by `preview_revert` or `prepare_revert`, and from version 21 `rescope_revert`. Shape details, from the descriptor alone: `SHAPE` (`INVALID_ARGUMENT`), `STORE_RESERVED` (`INVALID_ARGUMENT`), `FILTER_CAP` (`INVALID_ARGUMENT` `SCOPE_FILTER_CAP`). In `preview_revert` and `prepare_revert` (the agent's own proposal): `NOT_OWN_CHANGE`, `NOT_OWN_RUN`, `NOT_OWN_TASK` (the engine maps them to `NOT_FOUND` reason `CHANGE`, `RUN`, `TASK`, unknown and other agents' ids alike) and `FILTER` (`INVALID_ARGUMENT` `SCOPE_FILTER`; the message names each refused entry with the fixed texts of [MCP tools](#mcp-tools)). In `rescope_revert` a select that is not provably own work, or any select after a widen, is never answered with `BS011` before the proof or token check: it fails as `BS005` `REVERT_INVALID` detail `WIDEN_REFUSED`, one message for every cause; only a proven or own-work select gets `FILTER` or `DESCRIPTOR` (`INVALID_ARGUMENT`) for an agent, run or table outside the scope ([check order](#rescope-widen-and-reject)) |

## Passkey management

M4.1. WebAuthn gives the server no signal when a person deletes a passkey from a device or password manager, so Basalt never learns it on its own. Removal is explicit (`basalt passkey remove`, revoked in Postgres), `basalt passkey check` finds passkeys a device can no longer produce, and `basalt log` warns about passkeys unused for 90 days.

- **Revoked means refused.** `_verify_assertion` refuses a revoked passkey, so it can no longer approve a held write, sign a revert or sign a large plan. Revocation is admin only; an agent role can neither register nor revoke a passkey.
- **Page.** `web/passkeys/`, embedded in the binary and served by the engine's local pages and the portal at `/passkeys/check` and `/passkeys/forget` ([Page links](#page-links)). Static, under the same rules as the approval pages: no backend, no relay, no third-party code, nothing sent over the network, input in the URL fragment, and for a check a code the person copies. It runs only at the portal (RP ID `basaltvault.app`) or `http://localhost:<port>` (RP ID `localhost`), and a link is only built on the origin a passkey was registered with, since only a page on that origin can ask for it or signal about it.
- **Request.** Fragment `#1.<b64url(json)>` (not deflated: it is small), one of:
  - `{ "mode": "CHECK", "challenge", "credentials": [{ "credentialId", "label" }] }`: `challenge` 32 random bytes, base64url; 1 to 100 credentials of the page's origin.
  - `{ "mode": "FORGET", "removed": [{ "credentialId", "label", "userHandle" }], "accepted": ["<credentialId>", …] }`: `userHandle` optional (absent for passkeys registered before version 12); `accepted` the active passkeys of the same RP ID.
- **Check.** The page asks for one passkey at a time (`navigator.credentials.get` with `allowCredentials` holding only that credential, `userVerification: "required"`). An assertion means found on this device; any error (none on this device, prompt cancelled, timed out) means not found, and the page offers to try that one again. The WebAuthn challenge is `sha256(utf8("basalt-passkey-check.1." + challenge))`. Approval and revert challenges are `sha256` of a binding text that Postgres stored and that is a JSON object, so a check signature can never match one: nothing in Postgres accepts it, and only the CLI verifies it, to report the result.
- **Check response.** Text `basalt-passkey-check.1.<b64url(json)>`: `{ "challenge", "found": [{ "credentialId", "authenticatorData", "clientDataJson", "signature" }], "missing": ["<credentialId>", …] }`. The CLI matches `challenge` to the link it printed and checks each found assertion with the same rules as approvals (type `webauthn.get`, challenge, origin, `rpIdHash`, UP and UV, ES256 signature; the counter may lag, since check signatures are never submitted).
- **Forget.** For each removed passkey the page calls `PublicKeyCredential.signalUnknownCredential({ rpId, credentialId })` when the browser has it, and, when it has `PublicKeyCredential.signalAllAcceptedCredentials` and the request carries the passkey's `userHandle`, also `signalAllAcceptedCredentials({ rpId, userId: userHandle, allAcceptedCredentialIds: accepted })`. Both are feature-detected; a browser with neither (or a call that fails) is a no-op, and the page then tells the person to delete the passkey by hand in their password manager. Signals are hints: a password manager may hide or delete the passkey, or ignore them. The page shows what it sent and outputs no code.
- **Unused.** A passkey is unused when no approval or revert was signed with it for 90 days (`last_used_at`, else `created_at`); `basalt log` names it. Nothing is revoked automatically.

## PII classification

M5.1. Owner decisions M51-1 to M51-8. Every column an agent can read gets one class, for the whole database and every agent (M51-3). `basalt classify` suggests, the person confirms, `basalt_meta` stores it ([version 15](#version-15-pii-classification)). In M5.1 nothing is tokenized on the wire and nothing is refused: an unclassified column is only warned about (M51-5); refusals start in M5.2.

- **No value is ever printed, logged, written to the ledger or stored in plain form.** The CLI and the engine print counts, column names and reasons; the dictionary holds keyed hashes; `basalt_meta` holds classes, never values.
- Agents can read the classification and never change it. Only the admin writes it, through `basalt classify`.

### Classes

| Class | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `PUBLIC` | Leaves the machine as is |
| `IDENTIFIER` | One person's value, with an entity type: in the dictionary (M5.1) and tokenized on the wire (M5.3) |
| `FREE_TEXT` | Text that may mention people: patterns and the dictionary are applied to it (M5.3). Detection is deterministic: see [What free-text detection masks](#what-free-text-detection-masks) |
| `SECRET` | The engine refuses to read it at all (M5.2) |
| `UNCLASSIFIED` | No person has classified it: warned in M5.1, refused from M5.2. A new or renamed column starts here (M51-4) |

CLI spelling: `public`, `identifier`, `free-text`, `secret`, `unclassified`.

#### What free-text detection masks

Owner decision DD-1. Detection in core is deterministic: it masks only what it matches exactly or validates, and never guesses from context.

- **Masked:**
  - a value stored in an `IDENTIFIER` column, when free text holds it exactly, compared after normalizing (case and width), within known limits. The main ones: a value is found only when it has at most 4 words with at most 3 characters between them; only a two-word `PERSON_NAME` is also found in reversed order; a single common word never matches alone;
  - an email address, by its shape;
  - a phone number: a `+` number that libphonenumber says is valid; a `00` number that holds a separator and has a length its country dials, by its shape; and the US form `(202) 555-0143`, by its shape. A `+` number that libphonenumber says is only possible, not valid, is not masked, whatever word stands next to it (owner decision Q-14);
  - a 13 to 19 digit card number from a known issuer range that passes Luhn;
  - an IBAN that passes its checksum.
- **Not masked in free text:** a stored one-word value that is also a common word (`Rose`, `Hope`, `Price`), whatever stands around it (a title, a greeting, a capital letter, a JSON field); it is masked in its own column and as part of a stored full name (`Rose Lepik`). A name the database does not hold is not masked. Heuristics that guess names are left to an opt-in plugin.

### Entity types

Only for `IDENTIFIER`: every `IDENTIFIER` column has exactly one, and no other class has one.

| Entity type | Holds |
|---|---|
| `EMAIL` | Email addresses |
| `PHONE` | Phone numbers; the column also stores its phone region |
| `PERSON_NAME` | People's names |
| `ADDRESS` | Postal addresses and their parts (street, postcode) |
| `OTHER` | Any other identifier of a person: IBANs, dates of birth, IMEIs, account numbers |

CLI spelling: `email`, `phone`, `person-name`, `address`, `other`.

### Readable columns and the fingerprint

- **Readable columns** are the columns at least one member of `<prefix>_agents` may `SELECT` (`has_column_privilege(member, table, column, 'SELECT')`) and that same member has `USAGE` on the column's schema (`has_schema_privilege(member, schema, 'USAGE')`, from version 27; without it the role cannot reach the column): ordinary columns (`attnum > 0`, not dropped) of tables, partitioned tables, views, materialized views and foreign tables, outside `pg_catalog`, `information_schema`, `pg_toast`, temporary schemas and `basalt_meta`. Row-level security does not change this: a column is readable when the role may select it, whatever rows it sees.
- **Data type** is `format_type(atttypid, NULL)`, without the type modifier (`character varying`, not `character varying(255)`), so a length change is not a type change.
- **Fingerprint** = `sha256` over the readable columns as lines `schema.table.column:type:table_oid:attnum`, built with `format('%I.%I.%I:%s:%s:%s', …)` so names are quoted as identifiers when they need it (version 19; up to version 18, `schema.table.column:type` unquoted), sorted in byte order (`COLLATE "C"`) and joined by `"\n"`. Stored as `bytea`, shown as lowercase hex. It is the quick "did any readable column change" check; `pii_unclassified()` is the authority. One SQL definition, `pii_fingerprint()`, serves both `basalt classify` and the engine.
- **Renames and type changes** (M51-4). Classes are keyed by `(schema, table, column)` names, so a renamed column (or table) has no class and is `UNCLASSIFIED`; its old row stays until the next `basalt classify` removes it, and no view shows it meanwhile. A type change keeps the class and marks the column for review (`TYPE_CHANGED`) until `basalt classify` records the new type.

### `basalt classify`

```text
basalt classify [--review=changed|all] [--set schema.table.column=class[:entity-type]]...
                [--phone-region=auto|XX] [--yes] [--dry-run] [--json] [--check]
```

- **Credential.** The admin connection string comes from `BASALT_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL` or a hidden prompt, as for `basalt init`; it is used for this run only and never written anywhere. The CLI refuses to run when `basalt_meta` is missing or older than version 15: `basalt: guard schema vN has no PII classification; run basalt init again to upgrade it (same flags as before)`, exit `1`.
- **What it does.** Lists the readable columns, computes a suggestion and its reason for each (name rules, and a sample of up to 1000 non-null values read with the admin connection; values stay in the CLI's memory and are dropped after scoring), shows the review, and writes what is accepted.
- **`--review`** (default `changed`): `changed` shows only columns that are `NEW` (no row), `UNCLASSIFIED` (a row of that class), `TYPE_CHANGED`, flagged `needsReview`, or whose suggestion differs from the confirmed class; `all` shows every readable column. Columns not shown keep their class.
- **The review**, grouped by table: per column its name, data type, current class, suggestion with entity type, reason, and for a `PHONE` suggestion the region and its share (`parsed as ID numbers, 91%`). At a terminal, per table, the person accepts the table, edits a column (input `column=class[:entity-type]`, the `--set` spelling) or skips the table; a skipped table's columns stay `UNCLASSIFIED`. A column flagged `needsReview` is asked about on its own even when its table is accepted. The terminal layout is layout C, "summary first" (owner decision OR-4): a count line, then per table the columns grouped by class with `SECRET` first, each needs-review column asked on its own (`Protect <col> as IDENTIFIER? [Y/n]`; no asks for the class) before `Accept the rest for <table>? [Y/e/s]`; below 100 terminal columns the reason moves to a second indented line. This contract fixes what the review contains and the `--json` shape.
- **`--set schema.table.column=class[:entity-type]`** (repeatable): sets those columns' classes without the review; nothing else is written. The entity type is required with `identifier` and refused with any other class. A column that is not readable, or a value outside the spellings above, is a usage error (exit `2`).
- **`--phone-region`** (default `auto`): `auto` infers the region per `PHONE` column from its sample (the region where most values are valid numbers, weighted by the `+cc` prefixes seen in the database); `XX`, an ISO 3166-1 alpha-2 code (input case-insensitive, stored upper case), is used for every `PHONE` column of the run instead. The region is stored per column so the engine normalizes that column's values the same way. When the sample holds no value to infer from, the region is null and the engine normalizes only numbers written with `+cc`.
- **`--yes`**: prints the review, then accepts every suggestion shown without asking; a column flagged `needsReview` gets its protected suggestion and stays flagged. It never lowers a class a person confirmed: when the confirmed class protects more than the suggestion, the confirmed class stays. Without a terminal, `basalt classify` writes only with `--yes` or `--set`; otherwise it prints the review and exits `1` with `basalt: no terminal to confirm the classes; pass --yes to accept the suggestions or --set to set columns`.
- **`--dry-run`**: computes and prints the review, asks nothing and writes nothing.
- **`--json`**: prints the review as JSON on stdout instead of text ([`classify-review.schema.json`](api/mcp/pii/classify-review.schema.json)) and asks nothing: it writes only with `--yes` or `--set`, and `written` says whether it did.
- **Writing.** One transaction under `SET LOCAL ROLE <prefix>_guard` ([Writer](#version-15-pii-classification)), in plain DML: upserts the accepted columns of `pii_columns` (class, entity type, suggestion, reason, `needs_review`, `phone_region`, `data_type`, `confirmed_at`, `confirmed_by`), stores the suggestion and reason of every column shown but not accepted that has no class yet (class `UNCLASSIFIED`; a column a person confirmed earlier keeps its row), deletes the rows of columns that no longer exist (a confirmed class survives a revoked grant, owner decision OR-1), and sets `pii_catalog` to `pii_fingerprint()` and the time. It prints counts: `basalt: classified N columns (P PUBLIC, I IDENTIFIER, F FREE_TEXT, S SECRET); U columns stay UNCLASSIFIED`.
- **`--check`**: writes nothing and shows every column (as `--review=all`). For every `IDENTIFIER` column (the confirmed class, else the suggestion) it takes the sample and, in memory, makes each value's token, restores it and tokenizes the restored text again; it prints per column the values sampled and how many do not come back to the same token (`public.customers.full_name: 1000 sampled, 3 do not round-trip`), naming the common-word rule when that is the cause. No value is printed. Exit `0` when it ran, whatever the counts; with `--json` each checked column carries `check`.
- **Output.** No value appears anywhere. Reasons carry names and counts (`NAME:email`, `SAMPLE:EMAIL 912/1000`, `SHAPE:99% distinct, fixed length 12`, `KEY:internal id`), several joined by `; `. Exit codes `0` ok, `1` error, `2` usage.

### `--json` review

[`api/mcp/pii/classify-review.schema.json`](api/mcp/pii/classify-review.schema.json): `{ "fingerprint", "previousFingerprint", "review", "dryRun", "written", "tables": [{ "table", "columns": [...] }] }`, `review` `CHANGED` or `ALL`. Each column: `column`, `dataType`, `status` (below), `class` (the stored class, `UNCLASSIFIED` when there is no row), `entityType`, `suggestedClass`, `suggestedEntityType`, `reason`, `needsReview`, `phoneRegion`, and with `--check` `check: { "sampled", "roundTripFailures", "commonWordRule" }`. `entityType`, `suggestedEntityType`, `phoneRegion` and `previousFingerprint` are absent when they do not apply. Enum values as in [Classes](#classes) and [Entity types](#entity-types). The schema enforces that `entityType` is present exactly when `class` is `IDENTIFIER`, `suggestedEntityType` exactly when `suggestedClass` is `IDENTIFIER`, and `phoneRegion` only when one of them is `PHONE`.

`status`, why a column is shown: the first row that applies.

| Status | When |
|---|---|
| `NEW` | No row in `pii_columns` |
| `UNCLASSIFIED` | A row of class `UNCLASSIFIED` |
| `TYPE_CHANGED` | The stored `data_type` differs from the column's type now |
| `NEEDS_REVIEW` | Flagged `needs_review` |
| `SUGGESTION_DIFFERS` | The suggestion differs from the confirmed class or entity type |
| `UNCHANGED` | None of the above; shown only with `--review=all` |

### Engine, `basalt log` and `basalt init`

- **Engine.** At start and on the hourly upkeep tick the engine calls `pii_unclassified()` as the agent and, when it returns rows, logs `pii: N columns unclassified: schema.table.column, …` (names are not PII). It loads `agent_pii_columns` at start and keeps, with the map, the `pii_fingerprint()` and `agent_pii_catalog` (`fingerprint`, `classified_at`) it read in the same transaction. On each hourly upkeep tick, the same tick that calls `pii_unclassified()`, it reads the three again and reloads the map when any of them differs from the values kept at the last load: `pii_fingerprint()` changes when readable columns are added, dropped, renamed or retyped, or a table is dropped and created again (from version 19 its lines carry the table oid and attribute number), and the catalog changes when `basalt classify` ran. Between ticks, a result column whose table oid is not in the map (a table created again since the last load, say) makes the engine reload the map before it handles the values, at most once per oid per hourly tick, so a table with no classes does not reload on every query. A `(table oid, attnum)` still missing from the map after that is treated as `UNCLASSIFIED`: warned in M5.1, refused from M5.2. A move of `agent_pii_catalog.classified_at` (a `basalt classify` ran) reloads the map at the hourly tick, as above; the engine may also reload at once when it sees one, which is optional (M51-7). It never compares `pii_fingerprint()` with `agent_pii_catalog.fingerprint`: the two differ from the moment a column changes until the next `basalt classify`, which is what `pii_unclassified()` reports.
- **Engine files** in `stateDir`: `pii.key` (`k_master`, 32 random bytes, mode `0600`, engine user; created on first start, refused at start when its mode is wider than `0600`; M51-1), the directory `pii/` (mode `0755`, engine user), `pii/dictionary.bin` (mode `0600`, engine user) and `pii/status.json` (mode `0644`, counts and times only, so the person's `basalt log` can read it): `{ "dictionaryValues", "deltaValues", "fingerprintBits", "lastScanAt", "lastScanColumns", "lastScanRows", "updatedAt" }`, `fingerprintBits` `32` or `16`, times RFC 3339 UTC.
- **Engine config.** `engine.json` gains `pii: { "maxMemoryMB": 512, "scanRowsPerSecond": 20000 }` (the defaults). The engine sets `GOMEMLIMIT` to twice `maxMemoryMB` unless the environment sets it. `basalt service install` writes the service's memory limits from `maxMemoryMB` at install time: on Linux `MemoryHigh` (3×) and `MemoryMax` (4×) in the systemd unit. They do not follow a later edit of `engine.json`: after raising `maxMemoryMB`, run `basalt service install` again (with the same flags) so the unit is rewritten, or the engine may be throttled or stopped below its new budget. On macOS the plist carries `SoftResourceLimits`/`HardResourceLimits` `ResidentSetSize` with the same values, but current macOS does not enforce `ResidentSetSize`: there the limit is only `GOMEMLIMIT`, and the OS may evict the mapped dictionary's pages under pressure.
- **`basalt log`** prints one line on stderr, before its warnings, when the guard is version 15 or later: `PII: N columns classified, M unclassified; dictionary of K values, last scan <time>`. N counts the rows of `agent_pii_columns` with a class other than `UNCLASSIFIED`; K counts the dictionary's generic forms, so a two-word `PERSON_NAME`, stored in both orders, counts twice; M the rows of `pii_unclassified()` with `why` `NEW` or `UNCLASSIFIED`. When `status.json` is missing, cannot be read (permissions, an unreadable `stateDir`) or does not parse, the part after `;` is `dictionary not built yet`. It then warns on stderr `basalt: warning: M columns the agent can read are not classified yet: <names>; run basalt classify` when M > 0, and `basalt: warning: R columns need a classification review (type changed or flagged): <names>; run basalt classify` when any column is `TYPE_CHANGED` or has `needs_review`; at most 10 names each, then `…`. `--json` output is unchanged.
- **`basalt init`**, after installing, prints `basalt: N columns the agent can read are not classified yet; run basalt classify` when N > 0, N counted as `pii_unclassified()` counts for that agent's role (`why` `NEW` or `UNCLASSIFIED`).

## Plugins

Served in part. The engine serves the `engine.json` entries and the `basalt plugin` commands, signing and verification, the transports, and supervision: at start it verifies each enabled entry, starts it, checks its health, restarts it with a backoff and stops it, and `status` shows `PLUGIN_UNAVAILABLE` and `PLUGIN_NOT_TRUSTED`. `basalt service install` and the `basalt plugin` commands also write, start, stop and remove each local entry's service unit, OS user and socket directory in service mode, and `basalt init` refuses an agent OS user in the engine's group ([Isolation and trust](#isolation-and-trust)). Still planned: every hook's calls and messages (no hook is called yet), the ledger events of [In the ledger](#in-the-ledger), the engine connecting to those units' sockets (until it does, an engine run as a service starts remote plugins only) with the `PLUGIN_NOT_TRUSTED` cause that goes with it (an agent's OS user in the engine's group), and the health column of `basalt plugin list` (it shows `UNKNOWN`). The `PLUGIN_REFUSED` rows of [Error codes](#error-codes) and [Next steps per code](#next-steps-per-code) still read Planned, but the `basalt plugin` commands return that error as described. This section fixes the platform every plugin follows: the hooks, the manifest, the `engine.json` entries, consent, the transports, the protocol messages every plugin answers, failure, isolation and the ledger record. Each hook's own messages are added to this section by that hook's contract. Schemas: [`api/plugin/`](api/plugin/), with valid and invalid examples in `api/plugin/testdata/` and the ConnectRPC mapping in [`api/plugin/proto/basalt/plugin/v1/plugin.proto`](api/plugin/proto/basalt/plugin/v1/plugin.proto); the contract test (`api/plugin/contract_test.go`) checks every example and that the `.proto` has the same fields as the schemas.

### The principle

Core enables, plugins extend. Others extend Basalt without forking it, and core keeps only what Basalt's guarantees need.

| Plugins own | Core keeps |
|---|---|
| Where they run: a child process, a Unix socket, a remote host | The contract: hook points, messages, versions |
| Their latency, cost, uptime, model and hosting | What each hook may see and do |
| How they detect: patterns, heuristics, named-entity recognition, models | Failure: a data-path plugin that fails or misses its deadline fails the call closed |
| Their own credentials, in their own files | Consent and record: the person approves hooks, egress and agents; the ledger records them |
| | Signing and verification of what is installed |
| | The core floor of PII detection: exact dictionary matches and the validated email, `+` phone, card and IBAN patterns |

A plugin can only add. It never approves, reverts or signs, never lets through a write the guard refuses, never removes or changes a token core made, and never rewrites bytes on the wire. With no plugin installed Basalt keeps every guarantee it states elsewhere in this file: the core floor holds on its own. No `plugins` key in `engine.json` means no plugins.

### The lifecycle

The stages are store-neutral, so the hooks do not change when another store adapter lands. A plugin sees the stage, never the adapter's mechanism (a Postgres hold is `BS002` from a guard trigger; another store holds its own way).

```
engine   start → load plugins → serve agents → stop
action   proposed (intent) → checked → previewed → held | applied | refused → recorded
held     → signed by the person → applied   (or left unsigned until it expires)
revert   planned → signed by the person → applied | conflicted | rejected
data     store data and the person's text → wire to the AI provider (tokenized)
feed     every recorded entry, in chain order (cursor = chain seq)
```

Where plugins hook in:

```
person / harness ──request──▶ [ capabilities claimed → core floor → wire.detect plugins → tokenize → final scan (core) ] ──▶ AI provider
                 ◀─response── [ tokens → values in text, no plugin ]                                                      ◀──
ledger (per agent) ──▶ engine reads after the plugin's cursor ──▶ [ feed.observe plugins ]
held write, prepared revert, reissued link ──▶ [ approval.deliver plugins ] ──▶ the person
basalt classify ──▶ [ classify.suggest plugins ] ──▶ suggestions the person accepts or not
```

Never hookable: approve, revert, sign, the agent's SQL or store calls, writes to the store, the ledger itself, and the approval and revert pages.

### Per-agent scope

One engine serves many agents ([One engine, many agents](#cli)). A plugin entry sits at the engine's level and serves **only the agents listed in its `agents`**. A notifier listed for `product_a` and `product_b` never hears about `client_x`; a detector listed for `product_a` never sees `client_x`'s text.

- **Every call names the agents it serves.** The engine refuses, before any byte is sent, a call naming an agent the entry does not list.
- **Mixed sessions.** When one request to the AI provider holds text from several agents (one harness connected to several), only the `wire.detect` entries listed for **every** agent in the session run on it; the core floor always runs, with every agent's dictionary. An entry skipped this way is recorded in each of those agents' ledgers with reason `MIXED_SESSION`, and `status` warns: protection that dropped is never silent. The record and the warning are defined with the `detector/1` contract. The person splits the session to get every detector back. Running a detector on only its own agents' part of the text is not done: once the model mixes the sources, where each piece came from is lost.
- **Instances.** To keep one client's data out of a shared plugin process (no shared cache or model context), the person adds a second entry of the same plugin with its own `instance` and `agents`. On the engine's side two entries share nothing: each has its own connection, call slots, feed cursors and `auth.credentialFile`. Two **local** entries also run as separate processes with separate installed copies and sockets, and in service mode under separate service units and OS users; in same-user mode both run as the engine's user (no boundary, [Isolation and trust](#isolation-and-trust)). Two **remote** entries reach whatever runs at their URLs: if both name the same host, that host may share anything between them, and only the engine-side state is separate. An `instance` should not be another agent's name: it appears in the ledger detail of the agents the entry serves.

### Hooks

Four hooks in v1. A fifth, `wire.verdict`, is reserved. Hook names are the lowercase dotted strings below, an exception to the SCREAMING_SNAKE_CASE enum rule because they name a place in the lifecycle, not a state; transport names (`jsonrpc-stdio`) are lowercase kebab-case for the same reason.

| Hook | When | Sees | Power | On error or timeout |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `wire.detect` | Every request the engine's wire proxy sends to the AI provider, for a listed agent | Plain text of the segments no earlier step claimed | Add-only spans; core makes every token | The request fails closed (`DETECTOR_FAILED`, with the failure class) |
| `feed.observe` | After an entry is recorded in a listed agent's ledger | Event metadata, never row values or free text | None | Nothing blocks; delivery resumes from the cursor |
| `approval.deliver` | The engine holds a write, prepares a revert or reissues a link, for a listed agent | The notice: agent, headline, expiry and the short link | None over the action | Nothing blocks; the agent hands the person the link as without plugins |
| `classify.suggest` | During `basalt classify` | The catalog of the listed agents' readable columns, never values | Raise-only suggestions, never auto-accepted | Classify goes on without them and says so |

- **`wire.detect`** returns spans `{segment, start, end, type, role, confidence}` and the `model` it ran. Before any detector runs, core claims every piece of text that is itself a Basalt capability: approval and revert links (short and long) and passkey page links, request files and pasted request or response blocks (`basalt-approval.1.…`, `basalt-revert.1.…`), and setup codes (`basalt-setup.1.…`). They pass to the provider as without plugins, but no segment sent to a plugin contains one. Then, in order: existing tokens, core patterns, `NAME_CUE` plugins, the dictionary, `VALUE`-only plugins in config order. The final scan runs core only. Its message contract is `detector/1`.
- **`feed.observe`** gets events read from each listed agent's ledger through that agent's own access, after the entry's cursor (the chain `seq`), at least once and in chain order per agent; a plugin dedups by `(agent, seq)` and acknowledges the last `seq` it handled. An event carries `seq`, `at`, `agent`, `store` (`kind`, `database`), `event`, `runId`, `taskId`, `changeId`, `op`, `target`, `rows`, `cascaded`, `status`, `approvalId`, `revertId`, `reasonCode` and `chainVerified`, each when the entry has it. `target` is a table, or for an object or file store a bucket or folder, never a key or file name. Not delivered: row values (plain or tokenized), row keys, statements, intents, refusal messages and approval or revert links. A new cursor starts at the agent's chain head, with no history. Message contract `feed/1`.
- **`approval.deliver`** never gets the long link (it carries the preview in its URL) or the intent; a notice about a widened revert carries its headline only. The link lets a channel show the held rows; signing still needs the person's passkey, checked in the store. Installing it is a separate permission, shown in plain words: anyone who can read the channel can see the rows of held writes. Message contract `deliver/1`.
- **`classify.suggest`** gets schema, table and column names, types and column comments, limited to columns the listed agents' roles can read, and returns `{column, class, entityType, confidence, why}`. A suggestion may be stricter than core's, never looser; `--yes` never accepts one, and the review labels it with the plugin. It never runs in a process that holds the admin credential. Message contract `classify/1`.
- **`wire.verdict`** is reserved: a manifest that declares it is refused in v1.

### What a plugin never receives

Whatever its hooks, a plugin never receives: a database credential, the engine's or an agent's credential file, `pii.key`, the vault, the dictionary, tokens, column classes, which values are stored, the Vault channel's keys, an approval or revert link (except the short link in an `approval.deliver` notice), or the name or data of an agent its entry does not list. No log line, error, ledger row or plugin event carries a value a plugin saw or returned: errors name the entry (`name`, `instance`) and a failure class only. Two narrow exceptions: the ledger records the `model` name and version a plugin reports (short identifiers, [In the ledger](#in-the-ledger)), and `--plugin-debug` shows a plugin's stderr on the foreground engine's terminal ([Failure](#failure)).

### Manifest

`basalt-plugin.json` in the plugin's directory ([`manifest.schema.json`](api/plugin/manifest.schema.json)). A manifest is accepted only with a valid signature from a trusted key ([Signing and the trust store](#signing-and-the-trust-store)).

```json
{
  "manifestVersion": 1,
  "name": "acme-ner",
  "version": "1.4.0",
  "publisher": "Acme",
  "protocol": ["basalt-plugin/1"],
  "hooks": {
    "wire.detect": {
      "contract": "detector/1",
      "roles": ["VALUE", "NAME_CUE"],
      "entityTypes": ["PERSON_NAME", "ADDRESS", "acme-ner.EMPLOYEE_ID"]
    }
  },
  "endpoint": { "local": { "transport": "jsonrpc-stdio", "command": "bin/acme-ner", "args": ["--serve"] } },
  "files": { "bin/acme-ner": "sha256:…" },
  "network": { "egress": [] }
}
```

A remote plugin has `"endpoint": { "remote": { "transport": "connectrpc", "url": "https://ner.acme.example" } }`, no `files`, and its host in `network.egress`.

- **`name`** matches `^[a-z][a-z0-9-]{1,39}$`. **`protocol`** lists the protocol versions the plugin speaks and includes `basalt-plugin/1`.
- **`hooks`**: one or more of `wire.detect`, `feed.observe`, `approval.deliver`, `classify.suggest`, each with its `contract`. An unknown hook and `wire.verdict` are refused. **`wire.detect` may not share a manifest with `feed.observe`** (one plugin seeing plain values on the wire and ledger events could pair them to learn which values are stored) **or with `approval.deliver`** (a detector never receives links). `feed.observe` and `approval.deliver` may share one.
- **`endpoint`**: exactly one of `local` (`transport` `jsonrpc-stdio`, `jsonrpc-unix` or `connectrpc`; `command`, one of `files`; `args`) or `remote` (`transport` `connectrpc`; `url`, `https://` only, with no user name, password, query or fragment).
- **`files`**: every file a local plugin ships, by path relative to the manifest (no segment starting with `.`), with its `sha256:` hash. A remote plugin has none.
- **`network.egress`**: the `host:port` entries the plugin may reach, lowercase; empty means none. A remote endpoint's host, with port `443` when its URL names none, must be exactly one of them.
- **Entity types.** A plugin may return the well-known token types (`EMAIL`, `PHONE`, `PERSON_NAME`, `ADDRESS`, `OTHER`, `CARD`, `IBAN`) and types namespaced by its own `name` (`acme-ner.EMPLOYEE_ID`); a namespaced type with another prefix is refused, so two plugins never share a type or a token key. Namespaced types get a generic token shape and their own token key, and the person sees them at install.
- **`entitlement`** is reserved and refused. Unknown top-level fields are ignored, so a later minor addition does not break an older engine; a breaking change is a new `manifestVersion`.
- **Where each rule is checked.** The schema checks shapes, the hook rules and `remote` without `files`. Three rules JSON Schema cannot state are checked by the engine at `basalt plugin add` and at every start, and refuse with `PLUGIN_REFUSED` `BAD_MANIFEST`: the remote host is in `egress`, a namespaced type's prefix is the manifest's `name`, and a local `command` is one of `files`.

### Signing and the trust store

Every plugin is signed; there is no unsigned mode. A person building their own plugin signs it with their own key and trusts that key with one command.

- **The signature** sits next to the manifest in `basalt-plugin.json.sig` ([`signature.schema.json`](api/plugin/signature.schema.json)): `{ "keyId", "alg": "ES256", "sig" }`. `sig` is ECDSA P-256 with SHA-256 ([Primitives and encodings](#primitives-and-encodings): 64 bytes `r ‖ s`, base64url without padding) over the context line `basalt-plugin-manifest.1\n` followed by the manifest file's exact bytes, with no parsing or reformatting first. Any change to the manifest, one byte or whitespace included, fails it. The context line keeps a manifest signature from being any other Basalt signature.
- **Key ids.** A publisher key is a P-256 key; its id is base64url of the first 16 bytes of `sha256` of its DER SubjectPublicKeyInfo (22 characters), as for engine ids. The engine always computes a key's id from the key itself, for the embedded Basalt key as for every stored one; a file name or label is never trusted. Public key files are PEM `PUBLIC KEY` (SubjectPublicKeyInfo); private key files are PEM `PRIVATE KEY` (PKCS#8). base64url fields (`keyId`, `sig`) are decoded strictly: padding, other characters or non-zero unused trailing bits are refused. A high-S signature is accepted, as everywhere in this file; nothing uses a signature's bytes as an identifier.
- **What the signature proves.** For a local plugin: who published the manifest, and, through the `sha256` of every file in `files`, that no shipped file changed. For a **remote** plugin the signature covers the manifest only: who published its endpoint, egress and hooks. It proves nothing about what runs on that host, which can change at any time without the manifest changing. That is why the ledger records `endpoint: REMOTE` and the install screen says so ([Isolation and trust](#isolation-and-trust)).
- **Where plugins live.** Installed copies and the trust store sit side by side in one base directory, `<base>/plugins` (`<root>`) and `<base>/plugin-trust`. In service mode `<base>` is `/usr/local/lib/basalt` on Linux and `/Library/Basalt` on macOS: root's, outside the config directory, which may belong to the engine's OS user, so that user can never change what is installed or trusted. In same-user mode `<base>` is `<configDir>`, the directory that holds `engine.json`. `basalt plugin add` and `trust` create a missing `<base>`, `<root>` or `plugin-trust/` only after the path rule holds for the nearest directory that exists, each with mode `0755` and owned by the user running them: root under `sudo` in service mode, so `/Library/Basalt` and `/usr/local/lib/basalt` are root's and writable by no one else. A directory that appears while they create it is refused, never reused.
- **Trust roots.** The Basalt publisher key embedded in the binary, and the keys the person adds with `basalt plugin trust <public key file>`. `trust` shows the key id and asks to confirm, then writes the key to `<base>/plugin-trust/<keyId>.pem` and records `PLUGIN_TRUST_ADDED` in every agent's ledger. A key that is already trusted changes nothing and records nothing. It is refused on agent-operated engines and under an agent marker ([Consent](#consent)). A file in `<base>/plugin-trust/` that holds anything but exactly one PEM `PUBLIC KEY` block with a P-256 key, or whose name is not `<its computed key id>.pem`, is not a trusted key: a signature naming it fails verification (`NOT_TRUSTED` at `add`, `PLUGIN_NOT_TRUSTED` at start).
- **Author commands.** Neither reads or writes `engine.json`, the trust store or the ledger.
  - `basalt plugin keygen --out DIR` writes a new key pair to `DIR/<keyId>.key` (mode `0600`) and `DIR/<keyId>.pub`, and prints the key id and the public key's path. It never overwrites a file.
  - `basalt plugin sign <dir> --key FILE` checks the manifest in `<dir>` against [Manifest](#manifest) and every file in `files` against its hash, then writes `<dir>/basalt-plugin.json.sig` that verification accepts. It refuses a key file that group or others can read or write (it must be `0600` or narrower), that is a symlink, or that is owned by another user. Neither command prints or logs a private key's contents; their errors name the file, never its contents.
- **Verification**, at `basalt plugin add` (refused `PLUGIN_REFUSED` `NOT_TRUSTED`, or `BAD_MANIFEST` for a rule of [Manifest](#manifest)) and again at every engine start (the entry is not started and `status` shows `PLUGIN_NOT_TRUSTED`), in this order:
  1. The path rule below holds for the copy being verified (at `add`, the temporary copy) and for the trust store.
  2. At start, the installed manifest's `sha256` is the entry's `manifestSha256`.
  3. The `.sig` passes its schema, its `keyId` names a trusted key, and the signature verifies.
  4. The manifest passes [Manifest](#manifest).
  5. For a local plugin, every file in `files` is a regular file (not a symlink) with the stated hash, and the copy holds nothing else besides the manifest and its `.sig`.
- **Installed copies.** `basalt plugin add` first copies the manifest, its `.sig` and the listed files from the source directory into a new temporary directory under `<root>/<name>/<instance>/`, reading each source file once. It then runs the checks above on that copy, shows the consent screen from the copied manifest, records that copy's `manifestSha256`, and renames the copy into place as `<root>/<name>/<instance>/<manifest-sha256>/`. So what the person approved is exactly what is installed, whatever happens to the source directory meanwhile; a check that fails removes the temporary copy. The engine (and, in service mode, the plugin's unit) runs the installed copy. A remote plugin's copy is the manifest and its `.sig` only. `<root>` is `<base>/plugins`: `/usr/local/lib/basalt/plugins` on Linux and `/Library/Basalt/plugins` on macOS in service mode (written with `sudo`), and `<configDir>/plugins` in same-user mode. Each entry has its own copy, so changing one instance never touches another.
- **The path rule.** At every path component from `/` down to each installed file, and from `/` down to each key in `<base>/plugin-trust/`: no component is a symlink (a root-owned one above `<root>` or `plugin-trust/`, such as macOS's `/etc`, is followed), and no one but its owner can write it (no group or other write bit). Above `<root>` and `plugin-trust/`, `<base>` included, the owner is root or the required owner; from `<root>` and `plugin-trust/` down, the owner is the required owner. A trust store that does not exist yet holds no keys; the directories above it that exist still follow the rule. In service mode the required owner is root, with no exception for the config directory: that is why service mode keeps nothing of plugins there. In same-user mode it is the engine's own user, which is no boundary ([Isolation and trust](#isolation-and-trust)). So no user but the required owner and root can rename a directory on the way and put a tree of their own in its place.

### Config

`engine.json` gains `plugins`, an array of entries ([`config.schema.json`](api/plugin/config.schema.json)):

```json
"plugins": [
  { "name": "acme-ner", "instance": "default", "manifestSha256": "…", "enabled": true,
    "agents": ["product_a", "product_b"], "timeoutMs": 2000, "minConfidence": 0.6,
    "config": { "languages": ["en", "id"] },
    "auth": { "credentialFile": "/etc/basalt/plugins/acme-ner.token" } },
  { "name": "acme-ner", "instance": "client_x", "manifestSha256": "…", "enabled": true,
    "agents": ["client_x"], "timeoutMs": 2000 }
]
```

- **`agents`** is required: the agents the entry serves, each one of the engine's agents (`PLUGIN_REFUSED` `UNKNOWN_AGENT` otherwise). An empty list serves none.
- **`instance`** matches `^[a-z][a-z0-9_]{0,31}$`, default `default`. `(name, instance)` is unique, and every per-plugin path, socket, service unit, OS user and cursor is keyed by both.
- **`timeoutMs`** is the deadline of one call, from 50 to 60,000; default 300 for a local plugin, 2,000 for a remote one. It is the person's setting, not a latency budget: it only stops a plugin that no longer answers from hanging the engine.
- **`minConfidence`** (0 to 1, default 0): the engine drops a plugin's spans below it. Dropping only removes the plugin's own additions.
- **`config`** is opaque to the engine and passed to the plugin in `initialize`. At most 16 KiB (16,384 bytes) as compact JSON, and never a secret: a string in it, keys included, that parses as a URL with a user name or password, or that holds `password=` in any case, is refused before anything is written (the same idea as the `initArgs` rule of [Init saves its flags](#cli), applied to a plugin's settings, where a plain URL without credentials is allowed). A plugin that needs a credential (a chat token) reads its own file, as its own user.
- **`auth.credentialFile`**, remote entries only: an absolute path. The engine reads the file and sends its contents as `Authorization: Bearer <contents>`; the plugin never receives the path, and the contents never appear in a log or error. The engine reads it once, when it connects to the entry, so a new token takes effect when the entry starts again. The file must be a regular file (not a symlink) owned by the engine's user and readable by that user only (mode `0600` or narrower), at most 16 KiB, holding one token of visible ASCII characters; one trailing newline is dropped. A file that is missing, unreadable, empty, readable by group or others, or holds anything else is refused, and the entry is not started; the error names the file and the rule, never its contents.
- The engine reads `plugins` at start. `basalt plugin` writes `engine.json` under `<config>.lock` and prints the engine restart command.

### Consent

- **`basalt plugin add <dir> --agents a,b [--instance NAME]`** verifies the plugin, installs it and adds its entry disabled. Before anything is written it shows the person the hooks, the entity types, the egress hosts, `LOCAL` or `REMOTE` with the host, the signer and the agents, in plain words: a remote `wire.detect` plugin that declares `NAME_CUE` sees stored values in free text, so with it those values leave the machine; an `approval.deliver` plugin's readers can see the rows of held writes; a local plugin with a non-empty `egress` runs with network access, not enforced per host. Then it asks to confirm; any answer but yes, or no answer, writes nothing.
- **`enable`** and **`disable <name> [--instance NAME]`** flip `enabled`. `enable` first verifies the installed copy and shows the same screen from it, and asks to confirm.
- **Changing an entry.** `basalt plugin add <dir> --agents a,b [--instance NAME]` for a `(name, instance)` that already exists changes that entry: the manifest in `<dir>` replaces the installed one (a new version, or new hooks, egress or endpoint), and `--agents` is the entry's full new list. It shows the same consent screen, with what changes, before anything is written, keeps `enabled` as it was, and is recorded as `PLUGIN_CHANGED` (and `PLUGIN_ENABLED` or `PLUGIN_DISABLED` for the agents added or removed). There is no other way to change an entry's manifest or agents.
- **Agent-operated engines.** When an agent marker is set ([Agents and the admin credential](#agents-and-the-admin-credential)) or the engine is `operatedBy: AGENT`, `basalt plugin add`, `enable` and `trust` are refused (`PLUGIN_REFUSED` `AGENT_OPERATED`, exit `1`) before anything is written (the plugin directory's manifest is read only to compare its hash with the installed copy's): `engine.json`, the installed copies, the trust store and the ledger are unchanged. The one `add` allowed there changes an existing entry by removing agents from its `agents` and nothing else (same manifest bytes, no agent added). So any new entry, new hook, new egress host, changed endpoint (another host, or local to remote), new manifest version and added agent is refused. On those engines the agent runs the machine steps, so a prompt answered on that machine is no proof that a person consented. `disable` and `remove` stay allowed: they only narrow.
- The other commands: `basalt plugin list` (entries with version, signer, endpoint, isolation and health), `check <name>` (starts the entry, runs `initialize` and `health` and each declared hook with fixed samples that hold no store data, and prints the timing), `trust <public key file>`, and `remove`. In service mode every write needs `sudo`, as editing `engine.json` does.

### Transports

The contract is the messages, defined once; a **transport** is an adapter that carries them. The engine and the hooks do not know which transport is in use, and adding a transport is an adapter compiled into the engine, with no change to the hooks or existing plugins. v1 has two:

- **JSON-RPC 2.0**, newline-delimited JSON, one message per line: `jsonrpc-stdio` (the plugin reads requests on stdin and answers on stdout) and `jsonrpc-unix` (the plugin accepts connections on a Unix socket it is handed at start; the engine connects as the client). A local socket plugin, `jsonrpc-unix` or local `connectrpc`, is handed its socket as the one environment variable it starts with, `BASALT_PLUGIN_SOCKET`, the socket's absolute path; the engine connects once the socket appears. The socket's directory is new for each start: made by the engine's user under the OS temporary directory, mode `0700`, holding only the socket, never a directory the plugin's files live in, and removed when the plugin stops. A socket path longer than the OS takes (103 bytes on macOS, 107 on Linux) is refused before the plugin starts, naming the limit. Every local plugin starts in its own process group, with its installed copy as its working directory and no environment but that variable (a `jsonrpc-stdio` plugin gets none). A plugin must not write there: a file it adds or changes in its copy fails the next verification, and the entry is not started again; stopping it kills the whole group, so nothing it started outlives it. Methods are the message names (`initialize`, `health`, `shutdown`) and each hook's methods.
- **ConnectRPC** (`connectrpc`), over HTTP: Connect, gRPC and gRPC-Web, so a plain gRPC server works. Local (over a Unix socket) or remote. The service is `basalt.plugin.v1.PluginService` in `plugin.proto`, where each message has the same fields as its JSON schema, in snake_case.

**Remote endpoints.** TLS is always required: an `http://` URL is refused. The engine verifies the server's certificate against the URL's host, and refuses a certificate valid for any other host. The URL's host and port must be in the manifest's `egress`. Redirects are not followed, and proxy environment variables are not used: the engine connects to that host and port only. A refused endpoint is never started, and the error names the entry and the endpoint's host. The engine connects once to check the certificate before its first call, and calls with the gRPC protocol (binary protobuf over HTTP/2), which ConnectRPC and plain gRPC servers both accept.

**Local ConnectRPC.** A local `connectrpc` plugin serves on a Unix socket it is handed at start, as for `jsonrpc-unix`. The engine calls it with the same gRPC protocol over HTTP/2 without TLS (prior knowledge), so a plain gRPC server works. There is no TLS, credential file or egress check: the socket's ownership and mode are the protection. The engine connects to that socket path only (never TCP, no proxy, no redirects, no `Authorization` header) and, each time it opens a connection, refuses a path that is not itself a socket (a symbolic link to one included). Messages, size limits and failure classes are the same as for a remote endpoint.

### Protocol

Every plugin answers three messages, whatever its hooks ([`api/plugin/protocol/`](api/plugin/protocol/)):

- **`initialize`**, the first call on every connection. The engine sends `{protocols, hooks, config, limits}`: the protocol versions it speaks, the hooks it will call (the manifest's), the entry's `config` (`{}` when none) and `limits` (`maxMessageBytes` 4,194,304, `maxInFlight` 4, the entry's `timeoutMs`). The plugin answers `{protocol, name, version, hooks, model?}`: one offered protocol, and its name, version and hooks exactly as in the installed manifest. Any difference refuses the plugin.
- **`health`**, every 30 seconds while the plugin runs: `{status: READY | NOT_READY}`. A plugin that answers `NOT_READY` takes no hook call (`UNAVAILABLE`) until a later `health` answers `READY`; it is not restarted.
- **`shutdown`**, when the engine stops or the entry is disabled. The engine first stops sending the entry new calls and lets the ones in flight finish, then sends `shutdown` and lets a local plugin exit on its own. It kills a local plugin's process group once the plugin has exited, when `shutdown` fails or is not answered within the entry's `timeoutMs`, and at the latest 5 seconds after it began stopping the entry. The engine stops all its plugins at once, so it stops within 5 seconds whatever they do.

Limits: a message in either direction is at most 4 MiB, checked before it is decoded. At most 4 calls are open per entry; a fifth waits for a free one until its own deadline, then fails `TIMEOUT`. Each call's deadline is the entry's `timeoutMs`. Unknown fields in a message are ignored; a breaking change is a new `basalt-plugin/<major>`.

### Failure

A failed call has one of the failure classes `TIMEOUT`, `EXITED`, `BAD_RESPONSE`, `OVER_LIMIT` or `UNAVAILABLE` (a hook's contract may add its own). A plugin that fails (a failed `health`, a process that exited, a failed start or `initialize`) is marked `UNAVAILABLE` and restarted with a backoff from 1 to 60 seconds, doubling, back to 1 second once it has passed a `health`. Before each start the engine verifies the installed copy again ([Signing and the trust store](#signing-and-the-trust-store)). One entry failing or stopping never affects another, and a plugin failure never stops the engine from serving agents.

- **Data-path hooks fail closed.** While a `wire.detect` entry listed for an agent is failing or not running, every request of that agent to the AI provider is refused (`DETECTOR_FAILED`, naming the entry and the class), until the plugin is back or the person disables it. `status` shows `PLUGIN_UNAVAILABLE`.
- `feed.observe` and `approval.deliver` never block anything; `classify.suggest` lets classify go on and says so.
- **Stderr.** A plugin may see PII, so the engine never logs its stderr. In service mode it goes to the plugin's own log; in same-user mode it is discarded, unless the engine runs in the foreground with `--plugin-debug`, which shows it on that terminal only, never in a log file.

### Isolation and trust

- **Service mode.** Each local entry runs as its own OS user, under its own service unit, started by the service manager. It cannot read the engine's files, `pii.key`, the vault or credential files, and the agents' OS users cannot connect to its socket. With an empty `egress` it also runs without network, recorded `network: ENFORCED`. With a non-empty `egress` it runs with network access: the allowlist is declared, shown at install and recorded `network: DECLARED`, not enforced per host. On an OS where network isolation is not available yet it is recorded `NOT_ENFORCED`, and `status` warns. Until the engine connects to those units' sockets (planned), an engine run as a service starts no local plugin, so none ever runs with the engine's privileges: `status` shows `PLUGIN_UNAVAILABLE`, and `basalt plugin check` refuses it. The units, users and sockets are set out in [Service units](#service-units).
- **Never as root.** An engine running as root, in any mode, never starts a local plugin (`PLUGIN_UNAVAILABLE`), and `basalt plugin check` run as root refuses one.
- **Same-user mode is no security boundary.** In the foreground engine (development, CI) a plugin runs as a child process of the engine's user and can read whatever the engine can; an agent that runs as that same user can bypass the `basalt plugin` refusals by writing `engine.json` and the trust store itself, adding a plugin and trusting its own key. Only the ledger shows it happened (`PLUGIN_TRUST_ADDED`, `PLUGIN_ENABLED`, `isolation.files: NONE`), and the engine warns at start whenever a plugin runs this way.
- **Agents' OS users stay out of the engine's group.** `basalt init` (and `basalt service install --agent`) refuses an `--os-user` that is the engine's own OS user, is in the engine's group, or is named as a plugin's user (`basalt-plugin-…`, `_basalt-plugin-…`), the group named as the engine's user: that group reaches every plugin socket and the engine's config directory. Before the first install the group does not exist and no one is in it; a user or group that cannot be looked up refuses.
- **Remote plugins are trusted by declaration and record.** A signature proves who published a remote plugin's manifest, endpoint and egress, not what runs on that host. A remote plugin's own onward calls cannot be enforced; the declaration the person approved and the ledger's `endpoint: REMOTE` are the control, and `basalt plugin add` says so.

### Service units

In service mode the service manager runs each local entry, never the engine. A remote entry gets no unit and no OS user. Every name below is made from the entry's `(name, instance)`, so two entries, two instances of one plugin included, share no unit, OS user, group, socket, directory or process.

| | Linux (systemd) | macOS (launchd) |
|---|---|---|
| Unit | `/etc/systemd/system/basalt-plugin-<name>-<instance>.service` | `/Library/LaunchDaemons/dev.basaltdb.plugin-<name>-<instance>.plist` |
| OS user and its own group | `basalt-plugin-<name>-<instance>` | `_basalt-plugin-<name>-<instance>` |
| Socket directory | `/usr/local/lib/basalt/run/<user>/` | `/Library/Basalt/run/<user>/` |
| Network | none with an empty `egress` (`PrivateNetwork=yes`, `ENFORCED`); else `DECLARED` | `NOT_ENFORCED` |
| Stderr | the journal, under the plugin's unit | discarded |

- **The OS user.** Made by the install when missing (`useradd --system --user-group`, no home, no login shell; on macOS a hidden system account with a free id from 300 to 499 and a group of the same name and id), and named `BasaltDB plugin <name> <instance>`. A user name longer than 32 characters is shortened: the prefix, the start of `<name>-<instance>`, `-0` and the first 12 hex digits of the SHA-256 of `<name>/<instance>`, 32 characters in all. An unshortened name ends in its instance, which starts with a letter, so a shortened one never equals one. `basalt plugin add` refuses an entry whose user, on Linux or macOS, would equal another entry's, before anything is written. The unit's name is never shortened, so it always records the entry. Basalt treats OS users with these prefixes as its own, and `basalt init` refuses them as an agent's `--os-user`.
- **What the unit runs.** The command and `args` of the installed copy's manifest, from `<root>/<name>/<instance>/<manifest-sha256>/`, with the copy as the working directory. A manifest value that holds a control character or a line break, or an argument that is a lone `;`, is refused, never written into a unit; on Linux every argument is quoted with `%` and `$` doubled, so the unit reads back exactly the manifest's arguments.
- **Isolation on Linux.** `User=` and `Group=` are the plugin's, with `NoNewPrivileges`, `ProtectSystem=strict` (so the copy is read-only to it), `ProtectHome`, `PrivateTmp`, `PrivateDevices`, kernel and cgroup protection, `RestrictSUIDSGID`, `ProtectProc=invisible` with `ProcSubset=pid`, no capabilities, no new namespaces, private IPC, no `chmod` calls (so it cannot open its socket directory or socket to others), `MemoryMax` from the manifest's `resources.memoryMB` (1 GiB without it), only its socket directory writable, and `InaccessiblePaths` for the config directory, the engine's state directory, the engine's socket directory and every agent's credential file outside the config directory (a missing one is skipped). Unit files are readable by every user, so a credential file inside the config directory is hidden with it and never named; one an agent keeps elsewhere is named in the unit by its path. A remote entry's `auth.credentialFile` is not listed: the engine uses it only when it is the engine's user's alone. With an empty `egress` only Unix sockets can be opened (`RestrictAddressFamilies=AF_UNIX`). The unit is bound to the engine's (`BindsTo`, `After`, `PartOf`, and enabled as wanted by it): it starts after the engine, starting the engine starts it, and it stops and restarts with the engine. `Restart=always`, so after `shutdown` the plugin is started again and waits for the engine. On macOS file modes are the boundary: the plugin's user owns none of the engine's files and is not in the engine's group.
- **The socket.** The unit sets `BASALT_PLUGIN_SOCKET` to `<socket directory>/p.sock` and the plugin listens there, as in same-user mode ([Transports](#transports)). The socket directory is made by root at install: owned by the plugin's user, group the engine's group, mode `2750`; its parent `run/` and `<base>` are root's, mode `0755`. The unit's umask is `0007`, so the socket the plugin makes belongs to the engine's group, which may connect, and others, agents included, cannot reach the directory. The directory stays between starts.
- **Who writes them.** `basalt service install` starts the engine, then makes `<base>` and `<base>/run` and every local entry's user, socket directory and unit whose installed copy verifies; an entry that fails is named and left as it is. `basalt plugin add` writes the entry's unit (stopped while the entry is disabled), `enable` writes it again and starts it, `disable` stops it and keeps it, and `remove` stops it and deletes its unit, socket directory and OS user. A plist in `/Library/LaunchDaemons` loads at boot unless it is disabled, so a disabled entry's daemon is also `launchctl disable`d. If the unit step fails after `engine.json` was written, the command says so and names `sudo basalt service install`, which writes every unit again. `basalt service uninstall` stops and deletes every entry's unit, socket directory and OS user, by the entry's names, then removes `<base>/run` and `<base>` when they are empty: installed copies and trusted keys stay, as `engine.json` does.
- **A group-writable `/usr/local`.** Debian-family systems ship `/usr/local` as `root:staff`, mode `2775`. Then no directory under it can be made safely by root, and the path rule refuses every installed copy there. `basalt service install` still installs the engine, skips `<base>` and names the fix (`sudo chmod g-w /usr/local /usr/local/lib`); `basalt plugin add` and `trust` are refused (`PLUGIN_REFUSED` `NOT_TRUSTED`), naming the directory and the fix (`sudo chmod go-w <directory>`). The rule is not relaxed.

### In the ledger

Plugin events go through `record_plugin_event` into the ledger of each agent a change affects ([`events.schema.json`](api/plugin/events.schema.json)):

| Kind | Written to |
|---|---|
| `PLUGIN_ENABLED` | Each agent the entry starts serving, once (a restart writes nothing new) |
| `PLUGIN_DISABLED` | Each agent the entry stops serving, including an agent removed from its `agents` |
| `PLUGIN_CHANGED` | Each listed agent, when the version, manifest, hooks, egress, endpoint, isolation or reported model changes |
| `PLUGIN_TRUST_ADDED` | Every agent: a key added to the trust store applies to the whole engine |

- **Detail keys**: `name`, `instance`, `version`, `manifestSha256`, `signerKeyId`, `hooks`, `roles` (`wire.detect` only), `egress`, `endpoint` (`{kind: LOCAL | REMOTE, host}`, `host` for `REMOTE` only), `isolation` (`{files: OS_USER | NONE, network: ENFORCED | DECLARED | NOT_ENFORCED}`, `LOCAL` only) and `model` (`{name, version}`, when the plugin reports one; each matches `^[A-Za-z0-9._+-]{1,64}$`, so it is only a short identifier, never free text). `PLUGIN_TRUST_ADDED` carries `signerKeyId` only. The guard refuses any other key, and a detail over 4 KiB.
- The detail never holds a config value, text a plugin saw, or the name of any agent: no agent's ledger names another agent.
- A feed cursor is reset to the agent's chain head when an entry is enabled again, when an agent is added to its `agents`, and when a `PLUGIN_CHANGED` adds an egress host or moves the endpoint: history is never sent to a newly approved destination.

### Problems and errors

- `status` problems ([Next steps per code](#next-steps-per-code)): `PLUGIN_UNAVAILABLE` (an enabled entry listed for the agent is not ready) and `PLUGIN_NOT_TRUSTED` (the engine refused to start an entry listed for the agent: its signature, a file's hash or a path's permissions failed, or, planned with service-mode isolation, an agent's OS user is in the engine's group, which would let it reach plugin sockets). The message names the entry and the failed check, and a file only when it is the manifest or its signature: a local plugin runs in its installed copy, so other names there may be its own.
- The error `PLUGIN_REFUSED`, reasons `AGENT_OPERATED`, `BAD_MANIFEST`, `NOT_TRUSTED` and `UNKNOWN_AGENT` ([Error codes](#error-codes)), from the `basalt plugin` commands only.
